INTRODUCTION



Preface

The Public Accounts of Alberta are prepared in accordance with the Financial Administration Act and the
Sustainable Fiscal Planning and Reporting Act. The Public Accounts consist of the annual report of the
Government of Alberta and the annual reports of each ministry.
The 2024-25 Annual Report reflects the 2024-27 Ministry business plans, the Government of Alberta
Strategic Plan, as well as the ministry’s activities and accomplishments during the 2024-25 fiscal year, which
ended on March 31, 2025.
The Annual Report of the Government of Alberta contains Budget 2024 Key Results, the audited
Consolidated Financial Statements and Performance Results, which compare actual performance results to
desired results set out in the government’s strategic plan.
This annual report of the Ministry of Education contains the Minister’s Accountability Statement, the
ministry’s Financial Information, and Results Analysis, a comparison of actual performance results to desired
results set out in the Ministry Business Plan. This ministry annual report also includes:
    • the financial statements of entities making up the ministry, including the Alberta School Foundation
      Fund, for which the minister is responsible;
    • other financial information as required by the Financial Administration Act and Sustainable Fiscal
      Planning and Reporting Act, as separate reports, to the extent that the ministry has anything to report;
      and
    • financial information relating to accountable organizations and trust funds, which includes the Alberta
      Teachers’ Retirement Fund and school jurisdictions in the public education system (public, separate
      and francophone regional authorities and charter schools).
All Ministry Annual Reports should be considered along with the Government of Alberta Annual Report
to provide a complete overview of government’s commitment to openness, accountability, and fiscal
transparency.

Minister’s Accountability Statement

The ministry’s annual report for the year ended March 31, 2025, was prepared under my direction in
accordance with the Sustainable Fiscal Planning and Reporting Act and the government’s accounting policies.
All the government’s policy decisions as at June 4, 2025 with material economic or fiscal implications of
which I am aware have been considered in the preparation of this report.


[Original signed by]
Demetrios Nicolaides
Minister of Education and Childcare




Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025                                                                        3
INTRODUCTION



Message from the Minister

                                     Alberta Education and Childcare is committed to supporting the success
                                     of students and strengthening our education system. I am incredibly
                                     proud of the work we do in collaboration with our education partners to
                                     provide a world-class education for our children.
                                     During the 2024–25 fiscal year, Alberta Education made significant
                                     investments to reduce classroom sizes and build more schools for Alberta
                                     students and families. We improved our education funding model so that
                                     school boards are better positioned to respond to rising student numbers.
                                     We also introduced several new policies and programs designed to
                                     support student success and ensure we continue to maintain a well-run,
                                     world-leading education system.
In September 2024, we announced the School Construction Accelerator Program and made a generational
investment of $8.6 billion to build up to 100 new and modernized schools. This commitment means smaller
class sizes and schools closer to home—reducing commute times for students and families. Additionally,
Alberta Education created a streamlined funding process that allows these schools to be built faster. Since
beginning the program at the start of this school year, 33 school projects have already moved forward to the
next stage of the construction process.
In our classrooms, we have taken steps to strengthen learning foundations for our students. At the start of the
school year, we introduced a new policy removing cellphones and personal mobile devices from classrooms.
This change has helped limit distractions, allowing students to focus more effectively. We also enhanced
mandatory literacy and numeracy screening for students in Kindergarten to Grade 3, ensuring that children
needing extra help are identified early and receive the support they need. Beginning in the 2026–27 school
year, this screening will be expanded to include students in Grades 4 and 5.
This school year, we made significant progress in updating the provincial curriculum. The draft Kindergarten
to Grade 6 Social Studies curriculum was piloted by more than 2,100 teachers from 62 school authorities,
including a First Nations education authority. The curriculum is now ready to be implemented in
Kindergarten to Grade 3 classrooms starting in the 2025–26 school year.
Our proud tradition of offering Albertans a range of educational choices continued in 2024–25. With
funding for public, separate, francophone, charter, and independent schools, we provided more educational
options than ever before. We also made further investments to expand and enhance dual credit programs and
collegiate schools, giving students a head start on post-secondary studies and direct pathways to rewarding
careers.
In partnership with First Nations, Métis, and Inuit communities, we strengthened connections through our
Bridging Classrooms to Communities and Community Reconciliation grants. We also supported protocol
agreements with both the Blackfoot Confederacy and the Stoney Nakoda–Tsuut’ina Tribal Council. Work has
begun on a new Confederacy of Treaty 6 Education Work Plan, in collaboration with Advanced Education
and Indigenous Relations.

                                                                                                INTRODUCTION


Through the passage of the Education Amendment Act, 2024, we enhanced transparency and improved
communication with parents on public health measures in schools and important topics such as gender
identity, sexual orientation, and human sexuality. These changes will strengthen parental involvement in their
children’s education.
I am incredibly proud of all that we have accomplished and tremendously grateful to Alberta’s dedicated
teachers, school support staff, and our many education partners who help make it all possible. We look
forward to continuing our close collaboration to provide a world-class education for Alberta’s children.


[Original signed by]
Demetrios Nicolaides
Minister of Education and Childcare

INTRODUCTION



Management’s Responsibility for Reporting
The Ministry of Education includes:
    • Department of Education;
    • Alberta School Foundation Fund; and
    • Alberta school authorities.
The executives of the individual entities within the ministry have the primary responsibility and accountability
for the respective entities. Collectively, the executives ensure the ministry complies with all relevant legislation,
regulations and policies.
Ministry business plans, annual reports, performance results and the supporting management information are
integral to the government’s fiscal and strategic plan, annual report, quarterly reports, and other financial and
performance reporting.
Responsibility for the integrity and objectivity of the accompanying ministry financial information and
performance results for the ministry rests with the Minister of Education and Childcare. Under the direction
of the Minister, I oversee the preparation of the ministry’s annual report, which includes the financial
information, performance results on all objectives and initiatives identified in the Ministry Business Plan, and
performance results for all ministry-supported commitments that were included in the 2024-27 Government
of Alberta Strategic Plan. The financial information and performance results, out of necessity, include amounts
that are based on estimates and judgments. The financial information is prepared using the government’s
stated accounting policies, which are based on Canadian public sector accounting standards. The performance
measures are prepared in accordance with the following criteria:
    • Reliable – information used in applying performance measure methodologies agrees with the
      underlying source data for the current and prior years’ results.
    • Understandable – the performance measure methodologies and results are presented clearly.
    • Comparable – the methodologies for performance measure preparation are applied consistently for the
      current and prior years’ results.
    • Complete – outcomes, performance measures and related targets match those included in the
      ministry’s Budget 2024.
As Deputy Minister, in addition to program responsibilities, I am responsible for the ministry’s financial
administration and reporting functions. The ministry maintains systems of financial management and internal
control which give consideration to costs, benefits and risks that are designed to:
    • provide reasonable assurance that transactions are properly authorized, executed in accordance with
      prescribed legislation and regulations, and properly recorded so as to maintain accountability of public
      money;
    • provide information to manage and report on performance;
    • safeguard the assets and properties of the province under ministry administration;
    • provide Executive Council, the President of Treasury Board and Minister of Finance, and the Minister
      of Education and Childcare the information needed to fulfill their responsibilities; and

    • facilitate preparation of Ministry business plans and annual reports required under the Sustainable
      Fiscal Planning and Reporting Act.
In fulfilling my responsibilities for the ministry, I have relied, as necessary, on the executives of the individual
entities within the ministry.

Lora Pillipow

                                                                                                  RESULTS ANALYSIS



Ministry Overview

The Ministry of Education provides leadership, direction and oversight in the delivery of a student-centred
Early Childhood Services to Grade 12 education system that serves Alberta’s children and students.
The ministry is focused on:
• Student Success – Ensuring that the provincial curriculum provides opportunities for students to form
  the foundations for successful and fulfilling lives and supporting student mental health and well-being
  through collaboration across ministries. This includes monitoring student progress through comprehensive
  provincial assessments, including early literacy and numeracy screening, to identify and address student
  learning needs early on.
• Indigenous Education – Incorporating First Nations, Métis and Inuit histories, cultures, languages and
  perspectives in the provincial curriculum and collaborating with education partners to improve education
  outcomes for First Nations, Métis and Inuit students and help them throughout their learning journeys.
• Access to a Variety of Learning Opportunities – Providing Alberta’s students with a variety of career
  pathways to ensure access to post-secondary education or their chosen fields in the workforce and enhance
  their competitiveness in the modern economy. This includes enhancing career education programming,
  such as dual credit programs, and expanding access to collegiate school opportunities.
• Well Governed Education System and Workforce – Directing the education system and overseeing the
  administration of a variety of schooling options and education programs and services through legislation,
  provision of funds and planning suitable learning facilities. This also includes collaborating with school
  authorities to ensure teaching excellence.

Organizational Structure
The Ministry of Education consists of the Department of Education, the Alberta School Foundation Fund
and Alberta’s public, separate, francophone, charter and independent (private) school authorities. The ministry
leads an Early Childhood Services to Grade 12 education system that provides the foundation for all learners
to achieve provincial student learning outcomes, succeed in developing the knowledge, skills and competencies
needed to pursue their career interests and make meaningful contributions to their communities and the world.

RESULTS ANALYSIS


Operational Overview

Department of Education
Alberta Education provides leadership, direction and oversight of the education system, and supports students,
parents, teachers and administrators from Early Childhood Services through Grade 12. The department
provides leadership and direction by developing and supporting the implementation of provincial curriculum
for all subjects and grades, helping school authorities implement ministry programs and policies and enabling
excellence among the education system’s teachers and leaders. The department has a role in facilitating access
to francophone education and supporting school authorities in serving students with diverse learning needs
and improving outcomes for First Nations, Métis and Inuit students.
The department is responsible for funding the education system, and provides oversight by establishing
guiding legislation and policy, setting standards and assessing outcomes, ensuring accountability for financial
and performance outcomes and providing direction to school authorities to inform their planning and
reporting.

Alberta School Foundation Fund
The Alberta School Foundation Fund receives tax revenues from municipalities based on the equalized
assessment of real property in Alberta and education mill rates established by the lieutenant-governor in
council. In 2024-25, the education system received $2.5 billion in education property taxes (including opted
out school boards) to support students, parents, teachers and administrators from Early Childhood Services to
Grade 12.

School Authorities
School authorities include public and separate school boards, francophone regional authorities, charter
school operators, independent (private) school operators and independent (private) Early Childhood Services
operators. These school authorities provide programs for children and students in the province’s Early
Childhood Services to Grade 12 education system in accordance with the Education Act.




10                                                                             Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025
                                                                                            RESULTS ANALYSIS


Key Highlights
The Ministry of Education focused on accomplishing four outcomes identified in the 2024-27 Ministry
Business Plan:
• Alberta’s students are successful.
• First Nations, Métis and Inuit students in Alberta are successful.
• Alberta’s students have access to a variety of learning opportunities to enhance competitiveness in the
  modern economy.
• Alberta’s Kindergarten to Grade 12 education system and workforce are well managed.

Key highlights and results achieved by the Ministry of Education in 2024-25 include:
• Increased the number of student spaces by providing $2.8 billion for school construction as a three-year
  capital investment, funding 99 school projects in various stages across the province.
• Through the School Construction Accelerator Program, an additional $8.6 billion for school construction
  is committed, which will deliver more than 100 new and updated schools over the next seven years.
• Provided $9.3 billion in education system funding to ensure the Early Childhood Services to Grade 12
  education system continues to support children and students to develop their knowledge and skills and
  achieve learning outcomes.
   - In the 2024-25 school year, more than 794,000 children and students were registered in Early
     Childhood Services to Grade 12 education across Alberta, an increase of 3.1 per cent from the previous
     school year.
   - Revised the funding framework from a three to two-year enrolment average to address enrolment
     growth while protecting school authorities with declining enrolment. The new framework will be
     implemented in the 2025-26 school year.
• Released an updated Ministerial Order on Student Learning, focusing on career education, knowledge
  and skills development, educational programming and preparing students to be active and engaged
  citizens. To ensure alignment of curriculum development with the updated Ministerial Order on Student
  Learning, the department also released an updated Guiding Framework for the Design and Development
  of Kindergarten to Grade 12 Curriculum in April 2024.
• Invested approximately $31 million to support curriculum renewal to ensure Alberta students have the
  knowledge and skills to support their future success.
• Funded teacher professional learning and the development of learning and teaching resources to support
  the province-wide implementation of new Science, French Language and Literature and French Immersion
  Language Arts and Literature curricula for Grades 4 to 6;
   - Supported piloting of the new draft Kindergarten to Grade 6 Social Studies curriculum in 62 school
     authorities; and
   - Began engagement with teachers, school leaders and Albertans to inform the development of draft
     Grades 7 to 9 Mathematics and Social Studies curricula and Grades 7 to 10 Physical Education and
     Wellness and Career and Life Management curricula.




Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025                                                                        11
RESULTS ANALYSIS


• Introduced enhanced Literacy and Numeracy Screening Assessments for students in Kindergarten to Grade
  5 to ensure early identification of students who need additional supports.
     - Starting January 2025, Kindergarten children had their literacy and numeracy skills screened through a
       consistent, province-wide approach.
     - Students in Grades 1 to 3 identified as requiring additional support in January are screened a third time
       at the end of the school year to monitor their progress.
     - New screening requirements for Grades 4 to 5 students will begin in September 2026.
     - $10 million was invested in Literacy and Numeracy Support Funding to support literacy and numeracy
       skill development for students identified as needing extra supports through the screening assessments.
     - An additional $7.5 million was provided for Literacy and Numeracy Support Funding for 2024-25
       to further support school authorities in providing interventions to students who require additional
       supports.
• Transitioned the General Education Development program to the Canadian Adult Education Credential to
  support adults who do not have a high school diploma to receive the credential and open new doors to
  education or employment opportunities.
• Addressed classroom complexity by providing $42.5 million in learning support funding through the
  Classroom Complexity Grant for the 2024-25 school year.
     - Approximately $480,000 from this funding will be used to increase the number of educational assistant
       graduates in the province through the Educational Assistant Internship program. To date, 281
       participants have completed the program to become educational assistants or enhance their skills.
• Limited distractions in Alberta’s classrooms and protected student mental health through the Standards for
  the Use of Personal Mobile Devices and Social Media in Schools.
     - The new standards on personal mobile devices established restrictions, enforcement procedures
       and exceptions for Alberta’s Kindergarten to Grade 12 public, separate, francophone, charter and
       independent (private) schools and independent (private) Early Childhood Services operators.
• Supported school authorities in fostering positive student mental health by providing $20 million to the
  Mental Health in Schools pilot program.
     - Education is also working with Mental Health and Addiction and CASA Mental Health to expand in-
       school support for students facing complex mental health challenges.
• Continued to collaborate with Indigenous communities, partners and education stakeholders to enhance
  educational outcomes for First Nations, Métis and Inuit students.
     - $3.4 million was distributed to 12 grant recipients of the Bridging Classrooms to Communities
       Grant pilot program to help strengthen student connections and foster relationships with Indigenous
       communities and advance truth and reconciliation.
     - $250,000 was provided to the Blackfoot Confederacy and $250,000 was provided to the Stoney
       Nakoda-Tsuut’ina Tribal Council (G4) to support the implementation of protocol agreements.
     - $250,000 was invested to develop the first ever Alberta Education and Confederacy of Treaty 6
       Education Work Plan in collaboration with Advanced Education and Indigenous Relations.
• Expanded career education opportunities by investing almost $4.4 million into dual credit grants. A total
  of 79 grants were awarded to 60 school authorities to support 57 start-up grants and enhance 22
  enhancement grants.



12                                                                             Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025
                                                                                           RESULTS ANALYSIS


• Supported school authorities in recruiting qualified individuals to teach Career and Technology Studies
  while working towards their Interim Professional Certification through the Bridge to Teacher Certification
  program. Approximately $625,000 was provided to support 25 tradespeople to qualify for an interim
  teaching certificate while completing a university bridging program.
• Distributed the Home Education Grant of $1,802 per student to support more than 21,500 students
  enrolled in home education programs across Alberta and allocated $1.8 million in the 2024-25 school year
  to continue the Home Education Kindergarten pilot program.
• Expanded access to collegiate school opportunities to expand students’ pathways into post-secondary
  education and the workforce by:
   - Investing $23.7 million to support start-up and expansion costs for collegiate schools, supporting seven
     new collegiate school programs to begin operations for the 2024-25 school year. This increased the total
     number of collegiate schools in the province from five to 12, offering more than 30 unique pathways.
• Amended the Education Act through the Education Amendment Act, 2024 to support families and students
  navigating complex conversations around gender identity, sexual orientation and human sexuality, while
  also supporting continuity in a student’s learning during a public health emergency or state of emergency.
  New and amended sections of the Education Act relating to notification and consent for health measures,
  the right to education during emergencies and updated requirements for shifts to at-home learning came
  into force on March 1, 2025.
• Dealt with student transportation-system challenges by addressing ride times, transportation cost pressures
  and affordability, including reducing the distance criteria for student transportation eligibility.

The Ministry of Education remains committed to regulatory approaches and program delivery that reduces
unnecessary government oversight and emphasizes outcomes to improve access to government services, attract
investment, support innovation and competitiveness and grow Alberta businesses.

Discussion and Analysis of Results

Actions that support the priorities of the Government of Alberta Strategic Plan


     Key Priority Two:
     Investing in Albertans
     Objective three:
     Providing high quality education and opportunities for skill development
     Actions:
     • Allocating $2 billion over three years towards maintaining and enhancing existing school infrastructure and
       building new schools, to address the needs of Alberta’s growing communities. Detailed reporting found on
       page 65.
     • Supporting students with complex learning needs through specialized learning supports, and improved
       access to educational assistants and qualified professionals. Detailed reporting found on pages 37 and
       61-63.
     • Supporting relationship-building between Indigenous communities and schools, including providing
       guidance to First Nations education authorities, and using tools such as framework agreements to boost
       education outcomes for Indigenous students. Detailed reporting found on pages 36-38.
     • Enhancing career education programming and increasing collegiate school opportunities to ensure a
       variety of career pathways for students to enter post-secondary education or the workforce. Government
       is building a skilled, educated and engaged workforce for the future to prepare students for well-paying
       jobs following the successful completion of their programs, and address current and longer-term skilled
       labour market challenges. Detailed reporting found on page 49.
     Objective six:
     Forming lasting and meaningful partnerships with Indigenous communities
     Action:
     • Working with the federal government and First Nations, Métis and Inuit communities to improve access to
       key services such as education and healthcare for Indigenous peoples, no matter where they reside in the
       province. Detailed reporting found on page 36.




14                                                                                 Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025
                                                                                            RESULTS ANALYSIS


Outcome One: Alberta’s students are successful
Alberta Education provides leadership, direction and oversight in the delivery of a student-centred Early
Childhood Services to Grade 12 education system that serves Alberta’s children and students. In 2024-25,
the ministry continued to update and implement provincial curriculum to provide Alberta students with
foundational knowledge and essential skills needed for future success. The ministry monitors student progress
through administration of provincial assessments to ensure that Alberta’s Kindergarten to Grade 12 education
system remains accountable and responsive to students’ needs. Alberta Education also collaborates with other
ministry partners to expand mental health and well-being supports in schools and communities to empower
students with the necessary resources to thrive in learning and life.
Student success was achieved by:
• continuing to offer curriculum that provides students with literacy, numeracy, citizenship and practical
  skills;
• collaborating with other ministries to expand mental health and well-being supports for students; and
• developing and maintaining comprehensive provincial assessments, including early literacy and numeracy
  screening, to assess progress, identify learning challenges and monitor student success.

Key Objectives
1.1   Continue to offer curriculum that provides students with literacy, numeracy, citizenship and
      practical skills.

Curriculum Renewal and Engagement
Alberta Education is committed to providing students with a curriculum that drives their passion for learning
and prepares them for success in the classroom and beyond. In 2024-25, approximately $31 million was
invested in renewing Kindergarten to Grade 12 curriculum. This funding supported:
• implementation of new Grades 4 to 6 curriculum for Science, French First Language and Literature and
  French Immersion Language Arts and Literature;
• classroom piloting of new Kindergarten to Grade 6 Social Studies curriculum;
• development and procurement of learning and teaching resources aligned to the new curriculum;
• opportunities for teacher professional learning; and
• enhancements to the digital learning platform new.LearnAlberta.ca (curriculum.learnalberta.ca).

In 2024-25, Alberta Education continued curriculum renewal through an open, transparent and collaborative
process. As part of the curriculum engagement process, Alberta Education released an updated Ministerial
Order on Student Learning and the Guiding Framework for the Design and Development of Kindergarten
to Grade 12 Curriculum. The new ministerial order took effect on April 25, 2024, and focuses on career
education, knowledge and skills development, educational programming and preparing students to be active
and engaged citizens. The updated guiding framework sets clear direction for creating a developmentally
appropriate curriculum that upholds high academic standards and promotes personal excellence.
A new draft Kindergarten to Grade 6 Social Studies curriculum was released on April 26, 2024, for optional
classroom piloting in the 2024-25 school year. Alberta Education also began engagement with education
partners, including system leaders, First Nations education directors, subject experts and teachers to inform
the development of draft curriculum for Grades 7 to 9 Math, Grades 7 to 9 Social Studies, Grades 7 to 10



Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025                                                                           15
RESULTS ANALYSIS


Physical Education and Wellness and Career and Life Management. Out of 440 teachers nominated by
the Alberta Teachers’ Association and school authorities, 107 were selected to participate in the Teacher
Curriculum Consultation Group. They represent a diverse group of certificated teachers with a wide range of
subject matter expertise, including teachers from Indigenous communities, francophone communities and
all school authority types across geographic locations. This engagement approach helps ensure continuity
and alignment of proposed draft curriculum across grades as it progresses from elementary to junior high
school subjects. Feedback received from engagement will inform new draft curriculum in spring 2025, with
classroom piloting to begin in the 2025-26 school year.

Curriculum Implementation
In 2024-25, Alberta Education provided more than $14.3 million to school authorities to support province-
wide implementation of new Grades 4 to 6 Science, French First Language and Literature and French
Immersion Language Arts and Literature curricula. This funding included more than $6.7 million for
professional learning and more than $7.6 million to develop or acquire learning and teaching resources to
ensure schools have access to resources that are aligned to new curriculum and meet their students’ learning
needs.
Alberta Education authorized more than 220 learning and teaching resources in French and English
aligned with new Kindergarten to Grade 6 curriculum and facilitated 12 professional learning sessions
for education partners to raise awareness of resources and supports available on new.LearnAlberta.ca
(curriculum.learnalberta.ca). The bilingual platform provides a variety of teacher planning and collaboration
tools, including sample lesson plans and the Provincial Resource Review Guide to support the implementation
of new curriculum.

Classroom Piloting
Curriculum renewal funding in 2024-25 included more than $1.1 million to support classroom piloting
of the new draft Kindergarten to Grade 6 Social Studies curriculum. More than 2,100 teachers from 62
public, separate, francophone, charter and independent (private) school authorities, as well as a First Nations
education authority, piloted the new draft Kindergarten to Grade 6 Social Studies curriculum in the 2024-25
school year. Engagement sessions with classroom piloting teachers focused on professional learning to support
understanding of the draft curriculum and on collecting teacher feedback. This feedback was used to refine
draft curriculum before it was released for classroom piloting.
Piloting teachers were eligible to claim up to $1,000 ($200 per day for a maximum of five days) to participate
in professional learning and/or teacher collaboration meetings. School authorities were also reimbursed for
release time associated with substitute teachers. Department staff provided three virtual sessions in fall 2024
for piloting teachers to gather feedback, share experiences, support professional learning and identify areas
requiring further assistance.
Classroom piloting investments in 2024-25 included $350,000 in grant funding to The Edmonton Catholic
Separate School Division to support piloting activities and resource development and $220,000 in contracts
with The Edmonton School Division and The Christ the Redeemer Catholic Separate School Division
for the development of learning and teaching resources to support new draft Social Studies curriculum
implementation.
More than 500 learning and teaching resources were published on new.LearnAlberta.ca (curriculum.
learnalberta.ca) to support piloting of draft Kindergarten to Grade 6 Social Studies curriculum in English
and French.

                                                                                              RESULTS ANALYSIS


Financial Literacy
In 2024-25, Alberta Education continued to develop new curriculum to enhance financial literacy education
with the goal of providing students with opportunities for developing an understanding of economic
development, economic decision making and financial well-being.
Alberta Education continued to partner with Enriched Academy to provide financial literacy programming
for school authorities. In 2024-25, Enriched Academy received a $900,000 grant to provide Alberta students
with enhanced learning opportunities related to financial literacy, including money management, budgeting,
tax-free savings accounts and registered retirement savings plans, credit, student loans and saving across their
lifespan. As of June 2024, more than 360,000 Kindergarten to Grade 12 students have participated in these
learning opportunities. Student participation information for 2024-25 will be available in fall 2025.

French Language Education
Alberta Education recognizes the importance of preserving and promoting the French language and
francophone culture by ensuring francophone students have access to a quality education in French. The
department also supports French-language education through various programs, including French Immersion
and French as a Second Language courses.
Alberta Education supported the Official Languages in Education Programs by leading negotiations with
the federal government on the Canada-Alberta Agreement on Minority-Language Education and Second-
Language Instruction. In 2024-25, Alberta Education distributed $19.8 million in funding for francophone
education, French immersion programming and French as a Second Language courses.
The department also led the working group for the development of provincial standards and credentialing for
French immersion programming. The working group met twice in 2024 and will meet again in April 2025 to
advance recommendations that help promote the programs and incentivize more students to remain in French
immersion programs at the high school level.

Pan-Canadian Programs
Alberta Education worked with school authorities and the Council of Ministers of Education, Canada
Secretariat to administer the Odyssey pan-Canadian program. Odyssey participants, also called language
assistants, work directly in classrooms with French language teachers to provide students the opportunity
to interact with a native speaker, employ the language in daily life, and gain a deeper understanding of
francophone culture. In 2024-25, 78 French language assistants worked in 54 schools across four francophone
regional authorities and 20 public and separate school authorities.
Alberta Education also worked with the Council of Ministers of Education, Canada Secretariat to support
opportunities for Alberta students to participate in spring and summer pan-Canadian programs. These
programs promote francophone culture and expose Alberta’s students to francophone regions across Canada
while enhancing their French-speaking skills. During the 2024-25 school year:
    • 34 students were enrolled in the Destination Clic program;
    • 388 students from Alberta participated in the Explore program that occurred outside of the province;
      and
    • 221 students are registered to participate in the Alberta Explore sessions that will be held in
      collaboration with the University of Alberta, University of Calgary and Medicine Hat College.




Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025                                                                          17
RESULTS ANALYSIS


International Education
Alberta Education continued to embrace the diversity of the province’s population and support initiatives
that promote an understanding and respect of connections and differences between the world’s people. The
ministry contributes to building a globally skilled workforce by providing Alberta students with opportunities
to develop global competencies and intercultural skills.
In 2024-25, Alberta Education continued to administer student exchange programs (virtual and/or in-person)
with Germany, Italy, Japan, Mexico, Quebec, Spain and Ukraine. Through in-person programs, Alberta
students are paired with students from a region that speaks the language they are learning and alternate
sharing their homes, schools and communities with their partner for two to three months at a time. In virtual
programs, student partners participate in weekly online meetings over six weeks. Students take turns hosting
meetings and discussing various topics. Both in-person and virtual exchanges provide valuable personal
and academic experiences and provide valuable opportunities for developing intercultural awareness and
appreciation.
Alberta Education signed a memorandum of understanding (MOU) with the state of Hesse, Germany on
June 10, 2024. The MOU reaffirms the long-standing relationship for the education exchange program for
students, teachers and school administrators, and promotes partnerships between schools in both jurisdictions
based on mutual interests and shared goals in language learning, career and technology studies and other
areas of common interest. The agreement will be used to formalize regular connections, strengthen existing
partnerships and promote collaboration that will improve the education system of both jurisdictions.
The MOU will be effective for five years, with an option for renewal. By collaborating with international
jurisdictions to exchange knowledge about educational programming, Alberta’s government is better
positioning students to achieve academic success while increasing their competitiveness to pursue post-
secondary studies and high-demand careers in Alberta’s job market.

1.2   Collaborate with other ministries to expand mental health and well-being supports for students.

Alberta Education continued to support school authorities in providing mental health supports and
services that focus on prevention, promotion and early short-term intervention within the education
system. Additionally, the ministry collaborated with other ministries, such as Health and Mental Health and
Addiction, and partners, including Alberta Health Services and school authorities, to build an integrated
pathway to support access to appropriate health-related services and supports at school and in the community.
For example, a new specialized education program was established in 2024-25 in partnership with The Red
Deer School Division to support Recovery Alberta’s Step Up Step Down program. This program supports
youth aged 13 to 17 who have significant addiction and mental health concerns and ensures that they have
access to an education program while they receive treatment.

Mental Health Supports
Alberta Education continued to implement programs and services to support student mental health. In 2024-
25, the ministry collaborated with Mental Health and Addiction to enhance the integration of child and
youth recovery-oriented addiction and mental health supports in schools and communities.

Mental Health in Schools Pilot
Alberta Education continued the Mental Health in Schools pilot project to improve the delivery of mental
health supports and services for students and provision of tools, training and resources for the school
community. In 2024-25, the ministry provided $20 million to support 79 two-year pilot projects in school
authorities across the province. This included funding to extend 59 of those Mental Health in Schools pilots
that were initially set to end in December 2024, to continue until June 2025.


18                                                                           Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025
                                                                                             RESULTS ANALYSIS


CASA Mental Health Classrooms
CASA Mental Health Classrooms is a joint initiative between Mental Health and Addiction and Alberta
Education. In these highly specialized classrooms, students receive individual therapy, group therapy and
psychiatric care to support their recovery. The program is funded by Mental Health and Addiction and
delivered by CASA Mental Health, with each classroom supporting up to 12 students. As of March 31, 2025,
there were 20 mental health classrooms in operation.

Integrated School Support Program
Alberta Education continued to collaborate with school authorities to assist Mental Health and Addiction
and the Calgary Police Youth Foundation to expand the Integrated School Support Program. The program
provides school-based prevention and early intervention wraparound supports in elementary schools,
consisting of meal programs, after school care, structured physical education, a positive police presence and
access to mental health professionals. The program is funded by Mental Health and Addiction. As of March
31, 2025, there are 31 schools in 22 communities across the province offering this program.

Standards for the Use of Personal Mobile Devices and Social Media
Alberta Education took a measured approach to protect students by establishing Standards for the Use of
Personal Mobile Devices and Social Media in Schools, which came into effect on September 1, 2024. While
personal mobile devices can be useful tools to support learning, access to personal mobile devices and social
media can also negatively impact student achievement and mental well-being.
The standards were informed by feedback from more than 68,000 students, parents and education
stakeholders who responded to an online survey that was available from April 16 to May 3, 2024. Almost
90 per cent of respondents shared concerns over student cellphone use at school. Education partners,
represented by eight stakeholder associations also participated in virtual discussions with Alberta Education
on April 22 and 23, 2024.
The new standards on personal mobile devices established restrictions, enforcement procedures and exceptions
for Alberta’s Kindergarten to Grade 12 public, separate, francophone, charter and independent (private)
schools and independent (private) Early Childhood Services operators. Exceptions have been made for
students using mobile devices for health and medical needs, to support specialized learning needs and for
educational purposes. Individual school authorities were also required to develop and implement policies
that outline how exceptions will be managed and what the consequences will be for non-compliance at their
respective schools. The standards not only serve to reduce distractions in the classroom but also reduce online
bullying, maximize learning time and support student mental health.

Supporting the Safety and Well-being of Students
In 2024-25, Alberta Education continued to promote the safety and well-being of students by providing
resources and sharing best practices with school authorities and educators. Resources include educational
material to support teachers to recognize, prevent and respond to bullying in school; principles and procedures
to help schools ensure Grades 1 to 9 students are physically active for at least 30 minutes each day; and
information about peer support networks to help create a welcoming, caring, respectful, safe and inclusive
space for students.
Alberta Education continued to support the school nutrition program with an annual funding of $20 million,
providing more than 58,000 students across Alberta with a daily meal and snacks. In March 2025, the
Government of Alberta and the Government of Canada ratified the Canada-Alberta National School Food
Program Agreement. This agreement secured approximately $42.2 million in federal funding over three years



Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025                                                                           19
RESULTS ANALYSIS


to support the expansion of Alberta’s school nutrition program in the 2025-26 and 2026-27 school years
and ensure more students from participating schools are provided with a daily nutritious meal or snacks.
Alberta Education supported Ukrainian newcomers and their families by hosting information sessions on
Alberta’s education system in Edmonton and in Calgary on April 2 and May 6, 2024. More than 100 guests
attended the sessions and there have been more than 5,000 views on the Ukrainian Canadian Congress Alberta
Chapter Facebook page recording and live stream of the events. The department also worked with the Ukrainian
government to provide an opportunity for students from Ukraine to write the Ukrainian National Multi-
Subject Test in June 2024 – an entrance exam for Ukrainian students entering post-secondary institutions
in Ukraine.

Minister’s Youth Council
Alberta Education continued to encourage student voices and support student engagement through the
Minister’s Youth Council. For its 2024-25 term, 40 students from across the province came together in
October 2024 and January 2025 to discuss various topics with each other, the Minister and department staff.

1.3   Develop and maintain comprehensive provincial assessments, including early years literacy and
      numeracy screening, to assess progress, identify learning challenges and monitor student success.

Literacy and Numeracy Screening Assessments
Reading and math skills are important for lifelong success in and out of the classroom. Assessing student
progress during the critical early years can help teachers identify student strengths and areas where support
in literacy and numeracy may be needed to ensure the best possible learning outcomes for each child.
In July 2024, Alberta Education introduced an enhanced screening requirement following engagement sessions
with school authorities, education stakeholders and academic experts. Feedback on the existing approach for
early years assessments as well as perspectives on improvements helped in developing the enhanced literacy and
numeracy screening requirement. The twice-yearly screening for students in Grades 1 to 3 began in fall 2024.
Students in Grades 1 to 3 identified as needing additional support in January are screened a third time at the
end of the school year to monitor their progress. Assessment data submitted to the department indicated high
usage of government-provided literacy and numeracy screeners, with preliminary trends demonstrating that
an additional January screening was helpful in identifying students requiring supports.
• In numeracy, of those students identified as not requiring additional supports in September 2024,
  approximately 10 per cent were identified as requiring additional supports through the additional January
  screening requirement.
• In literacy, of those students identified as not requiring additional supports in September 2024,
  approximately 9 per cent were identified as requiring additional supports through the additional January
  screening requirement.

In addition, province-wide literacy and numeracy assessments started in January 2025 for children in
Kindergarten. New screening requirements for Grades 4 and 5 students will begin in September 2026. By
consistently screening the literacy and numeracy skills of students from Kindergarten through Grade 5, teachers
and educational professionals will be better able to identify gaps and provide targeted support to ensure student
success.
To support teachers in administering literacy screening assessments to students, Alberta Education added video
resources to new.LearnAlberta.ca (curriculum.learnalberta.ca), providing step by step instructions, examples
and visuals. A variety of other resources were made available for teachers and school authorities, including an
updated list of government-approved screening assessments, support guides, intervention lesson plans and more.



20                                                                             Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025
                                                                                                RESULTS ANALYSIS


In 2024, the Learning Disruption Funding was renamed the Literacy and Numeracy Support Funding and
expanded to include Kindergarten children. Through the grant, $10 million was made available to school
authorities for 2024-25 to support the literacy and numeracy skill development for students identified as
requiring extra support through the screening assessments.
In March 2025, Alberta Education announced an additional $7.5 million of support funding for 2024-25 to
ensure that schools have the necessary resources and staff to provide interventions for students in Kindergarten
to Grade 3, helping to prevent any student from falling behind in reading and math.

Student Evaluation and Assessments
Student evaluation and assessment is an integral part of the teaching and learning process. Assessment
information is used to improve student learning, enhance instruction to students and ensure that student
learning needs are met. While no individual assessment can provide a complete picture of a student’s knowledge
and skills, standardized assessments such as diploma examinations and provincial achievement tests provide
consistent measures of student achievement.
In 2024-25, Alberta Education continued to develop, administer and mark provincial achievement tests and
diploma examinations to report student achievement, ensure standards are maintained and assist schools, school
authorities and the province in monitoring and improving student learning.
Although the implementation of new curriculum in English Language Arts and Literature and Math for
Grade 6 students became mandatory in the 2023-24 school year, Grade 6 provincial achievement tests in
English Language Arts and Literature and Math were not administered. This gave teachers and students an
additional year to become familiar with the new curriculum. Instead, more than 59,000 field tests in these
subjects were administered between April and June 2024. The results demonstrated that students achieved
reasonable proficiency in the tested concepts. Alberta Education will administer Grade 6 provincial achievement
tests in English Language Arts and Literature and Math in 2024-25.
Similarly, Grade 6 provincial achievement tests for the first year of implementation of the new curricula in
French First Language and Literature, French Immersion Language Arts and Literature and Science will not
be administered in 2024-25; field testing of updated assessments occurred instead.
Alberta Education continued transitioning to a new digital platform for provincial assessments. The transition to
digital assessments is being implemented through an incremental process to allow teachers and students time to
learn and adjust. The 2024-25 school year was the second year of optional implementation of Humanities Part
A: Written Response diploma examinations and provincial achievement tests on the digital assessment platform.
Nearly all school authorities are now administering either provincial assessments or field tests using
the platform.

Canadian Adult Education Credential
Earning a high school diploma or equivalency credential is a key education milestone that provides the
foundation for future education, career opportunities and personal success. In May 2024, Alberta successfully
transitioned to the Canadian Adult Education Credential (CAEC) from the General Educational Development
(GED) program. The CAEC project began in 2019 under the Council of Ministers of Education, Canada.
Alberta Education led the development of the CAEC high school equivalency credential, working with other
jurisdictions to develop a fair, reliable and credible assessment that accurately reflects the skills and competencies
that employers and post-secondary institutions would expect from a Canadian senior high school equivalency
credential. Like the GED, the CAEC is for adults who do not have a high school diploma but need a credential
to open new doors to education or employment opportunities. The CAEC comprises five subject-area tests
in French or English: reading, writing, math, science and social studies. Eight provinces and territories have
signed agreements with Alberta to deliver the assessments. In 2024-25, approximately 3,500 CAEC exams were
administered in Alberta.

RESULTS ANALYSIS


Outcome One Performance Measures

Performance Measure 1.a:
       High School Completion
Rationale
• High school completion is important for entry to the labour force and post-secondary programs.
  While most students complete high school within three years of entering Grade 10, the five-year rate
  recognizes that it may take more time for some students to finish high school.
• Alberta’s high school completion rate includes students who earn a high school diploma or certificate
  of high school achievement and students who enter an Alberta post-secondary institution or an
  apprenticeship program within five years of entering Grade 10. Another group comprises those who
  have earned credits in a minimum of five Grade 12 courses, including one Language Arts diploma
  examination course and three other diploma examination courses.
Target
• The target was not met.

Discussion
• In 2023-24, 87.1 per cent of students completed high school within five years of entering Grade 10.
• The high school completion rate has increased over time by 0.9 per cent, from 86.2 per cent in 2019-20
  to 87.1 per cent in 2023-24 (Chart 1.a).
• Participation in diploma examinations was impacted by the COVID-19 pandemic. Diploma examinations
  were cancelled in April and June of 2020, and participation was optional during the 2020-21 school year.
  During this period, students who did not write diploma examinations received an exemption and their
  official mark was based solely on their school-awarded mark. In 2021-22, the January administration of
  diploma examinations was cancelled to support students affected by learning disruptions and the weighting
  of diploma examinations was temporarily lowered from 30 per cent to 10 per cent. The changes to the
  administration and weighting of diploma examinations may have affected the high school completion
  rates and caution should be exercised when interpreting the trend over time.
• The three-year high school completion rate is highly correlated with the five-year rate as most students
  included in the five-year rate complete high school in three years. For the cohort that entered Grade
  10 in 2019-20, 80.7 per cent of students completed high school within three years while 87.1 per cent
  completed high school within five years (Table 1.a). This makes the three-year rate a reliable predictor of
  the five-year rate in subsequent years. Projections indicate the five-year rate is likely to fluctuate over the
  next few years.
• Additionally, some students who do not complete high school in the Kindergarten to Grade 12 education
  system do so as young adults in the adult learning system. In 2024, 94.9 per cent of Albertans aged 25 to
  34 reported they had completed high school. This result has remained stable over time.

 Supplementary Information
       Annual Dropout Rate – All Students
 Rationale
 • The student dropout rate is an indicator of how well the needs of students at risk of not completing high
   school are being addressed by the Kindergarten to Grade 12 education system.

 Discussion
 • The dropout rate is considered a leading indicator for the high school completion rate.
 • Enrolment data indicates that 2.4 per cent of students aged 14–18 dropped out of school in 2023-24.
 • The dropout rate has improved over time.
 • The ministry continues to implement strategies focused on improving student, parent and community
   engagement in order to reduce drop out rates and encourage students to complete high schools.
 • Dropping out of school may be a temporary interruption of a student’s education. Of the students who
   dropped out in 2022-23, about 19 per cent resumed their education in 2023-24.
 • Some students who do not complete high school in the Kindergarten to Grade 12 education system do so
   as young adults in the adult learning system.

Performance Measure 1.b:
       High School to Post-Secondary Transition Rate
Rationale
• Post-secondary education equips students with valuable skills and knowledge to increase their
  opportunities for success and full participation in society. Entering post-secondary and/or apprenticeship
  programs indicates that high school students are prepared for further education and training.

Target
• The target was met, as the current result is not significantly different from the target value.

Discussion
• In 2023-24, 59.9 per cent of Alberta’s high school students made the transition to an Alberta post-
  secondary institution within six years of entering Grade 10.
• The six-year high school to post-secondary transition rate has remained stable over time (Chart 1.b).
• The four-year and the six-year high school to post-secondary transition rates are highly correlated for the
  same Grade 10 cohort. The difference between the four-year and six-year high school to post-secondary
  transition rates indicates that a substantial proportion of Alberta’s students delay their entry to post-
  secondary programs after high school. (Table 1.b)
• The four-year high school to post-secondary transition rate is also a reliable predictor of the six-year high
  school to post-secondary transition rate in subsequent years. Projections using the four-year and six-year
  high school to post-secondary transition rates indicate that the six-year high school to post-secondary
  transition rate will remain between 59 and 60 per cent over the next few years.
• Education continues to collaborate with Advanced Education, post-secondary institutions and industry
  associations to facilitate student learning opportunities in apprenticeship, skilled trades and vocational
  education.

Performance Measure 1.c:
       Overall Achievement Test Results in Grade 9 Language Arts and Mathematics
Rationale
• Overall student achievement of standards on provincial achievement tests is a sound indicator of whether
  elementary and junior high students are achieving high standards in Language Arts and in Mathematics,
  an important outcome for Alberta’s Kindergarten to Grade 12 education system.

Target
• The targets for the overall Grade 9 Language Arts and the overall Mathematics results could not be assessed
  as current year data is not available.

Discussion
• 2024-25 provincial achievement test results will be available in October 2025 and published in the Annual
  Report Update.
• In 2023-24, 69.6 per cent of students achieved the acceptable standard in overall Grade 9 Language
  Arts, while 52.7 per cent of students achieved the acceptable standard in overall Grade 9 Mathematics
  (Chart 1.c).
• Overall results in Grade 9 Language Arts are calculated as an average of the results in the following
  four subjects weighted by the number of individuals that were enrolled:
   - English Language Arts 9
   - French Language Arts 9
   - Français 9
   - English Language Arts 9 (Knowledge and Employability)
• Overall results in Grade 9 Mathematics are calculated as an average of the results in the following two
  subjects weighted by the number of individuals that were enrolled:
   - Mathematics 9
   - Mathematics 9 (Knowledge and Employability)

     publish Grade 9 Language Arts and Mathematics results as an indicator of student achievement related to the programs of
     study.
•    The percentages of students who achieved the acceptable standard include the students who achieved the standard of
     excellence.
•    Overall Grade 9 Language Arts results are a weighted average based on total enrolment in Grade 9 Language Arts courses.
•    Overall Grade 9 Mathematics results are a weighted average based on total enrolment in Grade 9 Mathematics courses.
•    See Endnote B, page 84, for information on assessing results relative to targets, and results over time.
•    See Endnote C, provincial achievement tests, pages 88-89.

Supplementary Information
      Overall Diploma Examination Results in Language Arts and Mathematics
Rationale
• Student results on diploma examinations are a solid indicator of whether high school students are
  achieving high standards in Language Arts and Mathematics.
• The Language Arts diploma examination measure is an aggregate of four Language Arts courses.
  This reflects a focus on fundamental reading and writing skills, an important outcome for Alberta’s
  Kindergarten to Grade 12 education system.
• The Mathematics diploma examination measure is an aggregate of two courses. This measure reflects a
  focus on the development of mathematical reasoning and problem-solving skills, which are important
  outcomes for Alberta’s Kindergarten to Grade 12 education system.

Discussion
• 2024-25 diploma examination results will be available in October 2025 and published in the Annual
  Report Update.
• The Language Arts measure is an average of the results in the following four subjects, weighted by the total
  number of individuals who wrote each of the exams:
     - English Language Arts 30-1
     - English Language Arts 30-2
     - French Language Arts 30-1
     - Français 30-1

• The Mathematics measure is an average of the results in the following two subjects, weighted by the total
  number of individuals that wrote each of the exams:
     - Mathematics 30-1
     - Mathematics 30-2

RESULTS ANALYSIS


Outcome Two: First Nations, Métis and Inuit students in Alberta are successful
Alberta Education collaborates with education partners to improve education outcomes for First Nations,
Métis and Inuit students by incorporating Indigenous histories, cultures, languages and perspectives in
curriculum, promoting foundational knowledge for teachers and school leaders and fostering knowledge and
understanding of First Nations, Métis and Inuit cultures among Alberta’s Kindergarten to Grade 12 students.
First Nations, Métis and Inuit student success was achieved by:
• strengthening education programs, policies and initiatives for First Nations, Métis and Inuit students;
• supporting partners to gather, analyze and share Indigenous education data to monitor student success; and
• enhancing relationship-building between Indigenous communities and schools through community-based
  supports.

Key Objectives
2.1   Strengthen education programs, policies and initiatives for First Nations, Métis and Inuit students.

In the 2024-25 school year, Alberta Education allocated $88.3 million through the First Nation, Métis and
Inuit Grant to assist school authorities in delivering system, program and instructional supports that improve
education outcomes for First Nations, Métis and Inuit students and in supporting the implementation of the
Truth and Reconciliation Commission’s recommendations.
Collaboration with Education and Ministry Partners
Alberta Education continues to collaborate with education partners to help improve their ability to deliver
programming and broaden their understanding of the overall education system to support Indigenous learners.
Alberta Education continued to support the Maskwacîs Education Schools Commission (MESC) and Kee Tas
Kee Now Tribal Council Education Authority (KTCEA) through existing framework agreements that outline
a shared desire to develop strategies to strengthen the delivery of educational services with the express interest
of improving the educational outcomes of students living and attending school on reserve. In 2024-25, almost
$3.9 million in Enhancement Funding was provided to MESC and $1.8 million in Enhancement Funding was
provided to KTCEA.
Protocol agreements establish a framework between the Government of Alberta and First Nation partners to
ensure a productive working relationship based on areas of mutual interest and cooperation. The agreements
are community driven and reflect the unique needs of individual First Nations. Education sub-tables under
these agreements have included work on culturally appropriate learning and teaching resources, land-based
learning and language revitalization and retention. To support the implementation of protocol agreements,
$250,000 in funding was provided to the Blackfoot Confederacy and $250,000 was provided to the Stoney
Nakoda Tsuut’ina Tribal Council (G4). Alberta Education also worked closely with Advanced Education and
Indigenous Relations to provide $250,000 in funding in 2024-25 to develop the first ever Alberta Education
and Confederacy of Treaty 6 Education Work Plan.
In 2024-25, Alberta Education established an internal ministry working group to identify pragmatic
approaches to address the five educational recommendations of the 113 Pathways to Justice report. The working
group is guided by the Alberta Missing and Murdered Indigenous Women and Girls Roadmap and seeks to
identify and develop evidence-based deliverables to support the government’s response to addressing violence
and increasing the safety and economic security of Indigenous women, girls and two-spirit plus (2S+) people.




36                                                                             Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025
                                                                                              RESULTS ANALYSIS


Specialized Learning Supports for First Nations Students
Alberta Education continues to address the needs of First Nations students through the Specialized Learning
Supports for First Nations Grant program. The grant facilitates the provision of customized learning supports
while building the capacity of First Nations schools to deliver those supports in a culturally competent and
safe way.
In 2024-25, $3.4 million in grant funding was provided to 29 First Nations and First Nations education
authorities to enable students registered with First Nations schools to receive support for social-emotional
learning and mental health services.

2.2   Support partners to gather, analyze and share Indigenous education data to monitor student
      success.

Alberta Education works with education partners, including Indigenous communities and school authorities,
to collect student data to measure student progress and success in the education system. The department
provides annual analytics and reports to provincial school authorities to measure how schools and school
authorities are performing, and to provide consistent, reliable data for planning and policy decisions. The
department develops data-sharing agreements between Indigenous communities, organizations and the
Government of Alberta to document how data will be collected, analyzed and used.
In 2024-25, Alberta Education received the research report for the Advancing Educational Equity in Alberta
Classrooms, Schools and Structures research project. This project, supported by the department through
funding contribution and in-kind support, investigated the systemic factors that contribute to the academic
streaming of Indigenous students into academic pathways that may limit post-secondary opportunities.
Recommendations will be reviewed and considered by the department for further action and implementation.

2.3   Enhance relationship-building between Indigenous communities and schools through
      community-based supports.

Alberta Education collaborates with Indigenous communities, partners and education stakeholders to enhance
education outcomes for First Nations, Métis and Inuit students by implementing initiatives that support
reconciliation, recognizing that Indigenous-led solutions result in meaningful change. Establishing strong
relationships with Alberta’s Indigenous communities can help schools provide Indigenous students with the
supports needed to be successful in school and beyond.

Bridging Classrooms to Communities Grant
The Bridging Classrooms to Communities Grant pilot program helps Indigenous communities and school
authorities build stronger relationships to support student success and reconciliation by creating welcoming
environments, demonstrating cultural awareness and providing system supports to encourage Indigenous
students’ success in and out of the classroom. In 2024-25, $3.4 million was distributed to 12 grant recipients
to help strengthen student connections and foster relationships with Indigenous communities. Grant funding
can be used for out-of-school programming, such as homework clubs, student drop-in programs, social clubs,
nutrition programs, sports programs and culture and language programs to help students develop healthy
relationships in their communities and provide a network of social and academic supports for students out-
of-school. Education continued to conduct bi-annual check-in meetings with grant recipients throughout
2024-25; the majority reported positive results from initial activities. The final report is expected by March
31, 2027.




Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025                                                                         37
RESULTS ANALYSIS


Community Reconciliation Grant
The Community Reconciliation Grant pilot program was launched in October 2023 to promote community
healing and reconciliation by fostering engagement between Indigenous organizations and schools to support
students through a holistic approach. Through an investment of $700,000, the program supports Indigenous
organizations to provide services and supports to children and youth from ages 3 to 19. The services and
supports include providing family support and community development, social and leadership opportunities
and cultural learning. Eight Indigenous organizations were awarded a conditional grant of up to $100,000
each to support their respective reconciliation-related project or initiative. The department remains steadfast
in its commitment to truth and reconciliation and results from this pilot will be used to inform future
initiatives and programming.

Strengthening Relationships Pilot Grant
The Strengthening Relationships Pilot Grant was a one-time initiative launched in March 2023 to foster
meaningful relationships between provincial schools and Indigenous communities. The program aimed
to create welcoming, respectful and culturally responsive learning environments that enhance Indigenous
student success. It focused on four key areas: Relational Supports and Community Development, Restorative
Practices, Social and Recreational Activities and Culture and Language.

Six First Nations and one Métis Settlement each received $140,000, totaling $980,000 in funding. The pilot
concluded on August 31, 2024. Reported outcomes included:

     • Increased engagement of teachers and school leaders with Indigenous communities;
     • Enhanced cultural awareness and integration in educational practices;
     • Improved student attendance and learning engagement;
     • Greater involvement of parents, Elders and community members in schools; and
     • Learning environments more reflective of Indigenous histories, languages and worldviews.
Results and learnings from the Strengthening Relationships Pilot Grant program are being used to inform
current and future ministry initiatives that support truth and reconciliation and improve Indigenous student
outcomes.

United for Literacy
In 2024-25, Alberta Education provided $800,000 to United for Literacy to plan, coordinate and deliver
seven literacy programs for First Nations, Métis and Inuit students in Alberta, including summer literacy
programs, rural literacy programs and urban literacy programs. Up to 21 summer literacy camps and 13
after school programs were held in 2024. United For Literacy is working on building relationships with new
communities and looking for opportunities for summer camp locations to convert to full-year programming.

Rupertsland Institute Grant
The Rupertsland Institute is an affiliate of the Métis Nation of Alberta. A grant of $500,000 per year supports
the Education and Training Strategy under the Métis Nation of Alberta – Government of Alberta Framework
Agreement until March 31, 2028. The grant enables the development of Métis-specific Kindergarten to
Grade 12 lesson plans, the creation of classroom resources and professional development to support Métis
foundational knowledge, and support for the implementation of the Teaching Quality Standard and raises
awareness among educators and system leaders across Alberta about the impact of academic streaming on
Indigenous learners.




38                                                                             Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025
                                                                                           RESULTS ANALYSIS


This grant has resulted in:
    • more than 34,500 educator accessing Rupertsland Institute’s online educational supports;
    • almost 650 educators completing the Métis Foundational Knowledge Themes course;
    • over 20 school divisions participating in the Niya Michif (traditional Métis language) course;
    • a robust online platform featuring more than 55 lesson plans and more than 15 virtual field trips in
      both English and French; and
    • educators reporting increased confidence and competence in integrating Métis content into classroom
      instruction.




Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025                                                                        39
RESULTS ANALYSIS


Outcome Two Performance Measures

Performance Measure 2.a:
      High School Completion of Self-Identified First Nations, Métis and Inuit Students

Rationale
• High school completion of self-identified First Nations, Métis and Inuit students is important for entry
  to the labour force and post-secondary programs. While most self-identified First Nations, Métis and Inuit
  students complete high school within three years of entering Grade 10, the five-year rate recognizes that it
  may take more time for some self-identified First Nations, Métis and Inuit students to finish high school.
• Alberta’s high school completion rate of self-identified First Nations, Métis and Inuit students includes
  students who earn a high school diploma or certificate of high school achievement and self-identified First
  Nations, Métis and Inuit students who enter an Alberta post-secondary institution or an apprenticeship
  program within five years of entering Grade 10. Another group comprises those who have earned credits in
  a minimum of five Grade 12 courses, including one Language Arts diploma examination course and three
  other diploma examination courses.

Target
• The target was not met.

Discussion
• In 2023-24, 69.7 per cent of self-identified First Nations, Métis and Inuit students completed high school
  within five years of entering Grade 10.
• The high school completion rate of self-identified First Nations, Métis and Inuit students has remained
  relatively stable over time, from 68.1 per cent in 2019-20 to 69.7 per cent in 2023-24 (Chart 2.a).
• Participation in diploma examinations was impacted by the COVID-19 pandemic. Diploma examinations
  were cancelled in April and June 2020 and participation was optional during the 2020-21 school year.
  During this period, students who did not write diploma examinations received an exemption and their
  official mark was based solely on their school-awarded mark. In 2021-22, the January administration of
  diploma examinations was cancelled to support students affected by learning disruptions and the weighting
  of diploma examinations was temporarily lowered from 30 per cent to 10 per cent. These changes to the
  administration and weighting of diploma examinations may have affected the high school completion rates
  and thus caution should be exercised when interpreting the trend over time.
• The three-year high school completion rate is highly correlated with the five-year rate, as most students in
  the five-year rate complete high school in three years. For the cohort that entered Grade 10 in 2019-20,
  57.0 per cent of students completed high school within three years while 69.7 per cent completed high
  school within five years (Table 2.a). This makes the three-year rate a reliable predictor of the five-year rate
  in subsequent years. Projections indicate the five-year rate is likely to fluctuate over the next few years.
• Additionally, some students who do not complete high school in the Kindergarten to Grade 12 education
  system do so as young adults in the adult learning system. In 2024, 88.6 per cent of Indigenous Albertans
  aged 25 to 34 reported they had completed high school.

Supplementary Information
     Annual Dropout Rate of Self-Identified First Nations, Métis and Inuit Students

Rationale
• The student dropout rate is an indicator of how well the needs of self-identified First Nations, Métis and
  Inuit students at risk of not completing high school are being addressed by the Kindergarten to Grade 12
  education system.

Discussion
• The student dropout rate is considered a leading indicator for the high school completion rate.
• Enrolment data indicates that 5.2 per cent of self-identified First Nations, Métis and Inuit students aged
  14–18 dropped out of school in 2023-24.
• The dropout rate for self-identified First Nations, Métis and Inuit students has remained stable over time.
• The dropout rate for self-identified First Nations, Métis and Inuit students is approximately twice the rate
  for students overall, which is 2.4 per cent for the same period.
• The ministry continues to implement strategies focused on improving First Nations, Métis and Inuit
  student, parent and community engagement in order to reduce drop out rates and encourage First Nations,
  Métis and Inuit students to complete high school.
• Dropping out of school may be a temporary interruption of a student’s education. Of the self-identified
  First Nations, Métis and Inuit students who dropped out in 2022-23, about 17 per cent resumed their
  education in 2023-24.
• Some self-identified First Nations, Métis and Inuit students who do not complete high school in the
  Kindergarten to Grade 12 education system do so as young adults in the adult learning system.
