      completed high school are included in the annual dropout rate. For example, self-identified First Nations, Métis and
      Inuit students aged 14-18 in 2022-23 were tracked for one year. Those who were not enrolled and had not completed are
      included in the 2023-24 dropout rate.
 •    See Endnote C, page 86, for more information on the calculation of self-identified First Nations, Métis, and Inuit Annual
      Dropout Rate.




 Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025                                                                                            43
RESULTS ANALYSIS


Performance Measure 2.b:
      High School to Post-Secondary Transition Rate of Self-Identified First Nations, Métis and Inuit
      Students

Rationale
• Post-secondary education equips self-identified First Nations, Métis and Inuit students with valuable skills
  and knowledge to increase their opportunities for success and full participation in society. Entering post-
  secondary and/or apprenticeship programs indicates that self-identified First Nations, Métis and Inuit high
  school students are prepared for further education and training.

Target
• The target was met.

Discussion
• In 2023-24, 37.1 per cent of Alberta’s self-identified First Nations, Métis and Inuit high school students
  made the transition to an Alberta post-secondary institution within six years of entering Grade 10.
• The six-year high school to post-secondary transition rate has remained stable over time (Chart 2.b).
• The difference between the four-year and six-year high school to post-secondary transition rates indicates
  that a substantial proportion of self-identified First Nations, Métis and Inuit students delay their entry to
  post-secondary programs after completing high school (Table 2.b).
• The six-year high school to post-secondary transition rate for self-identified First Nations, Métis and Inuit
  students is slightly more than half the six-year high school to post-secondary transition rate for all Alberta
  students (59.9 per cent).
• The four-year and six-year high school to post-secondary transition rates for self-identified First Nations,
  Métis and Inuit students are highly correlated for the same Grade 10 cohort and the four-year high school
  to post-secondary transition rate is thus a reliable predictor of the six-year high school to post-secondary
  transition rate in subsequent years. Projections indicate that the six-year high school to post-secondary
  transition rate will remain between 35 and 38 per cent over the next few years.
• Education continues to collaborate with Advanced Education, post-secondary institutions and industry
  associations to facilitate student learning opportunities in apprenticeship, skilled trades and vocational
  education.

RESULTS ANALYSIS


Outcome Three: Alberta’s students have access to a variety of learning opportunities
to enhance competitiveness in the modern economy
Alberta’s government is working to ensure that students have access to practical, foundational learning
opportunities while helping to build a qualified, educated and engaged workforce to support Alberta’s
economic prosperity. Alberta Education provides students with innovative learning opportunities such
as enhanced career education programming, dual credit programs and collegiate schools to ensure diverse
pathways for students to access post-secondary education or enter their chosen field of work, enabling
them to lead successful and fulfilling lives.
Ensuring Alberta’s students have access to a variety of learning opportunities was achieved by:
• providing career education programming that enables students to explore and experience various
  occupations;
• exploring strategies to enhance awareness of career learning pathways that include the apprenticeship
  system for students interested in pursuing training in the skilled trades, in partnership with other
  ministries; and
• continuing to support collegiate school opportunities to create more pathways for students to post-
  secondary education and the workforce.

Key Objectives
3.1   Provide career education programming that enables students to explore and experience various
      occupations.

Career education programming enables students to have hands-on, experiential learning experiences that
can lead to rewarding careers. To ensure students are prepared to participate in Alberta’s economy, Alberta
Education supports students to explore educational pathways to post-secondary education, trade designation
and meaningful careers through career education programming.

Career Education Engagement
Alberta Education engaged education partners and teachers on the development of draft curriculum for future
Grades 7 to 10 courses in January and February 2025, including content on physical education and wellness,
career education, financial literacy and additional basic life skills and home maintenance. These partners also
provided input on the organization of content into courses to best support student success. Partners confirmed
that career education and financial literacy are important in junior and senior high school and noted that
additional time to consider the high school context is needed.
Based on this feedback, Alberta Education is moving forward with additional engagement on Grades 7 to
9 draft curriculum related to career education and financial literacy. Work is also underway to develop high
school curriculum for Career and Life Management, including content in career education, financial literacy,
basic life skills and home maintenance, to be released for engagement at a later date. Input from engagement
activities will build on existing career education options in Alberta.
In collaboration with Advanced Education, Alberta Education also established a Dual Credit Review Advisory
Group in June 2024 to review several components of Alberta’s dual credit programming. The advisory group
included representatives from school authorities across Alberta, Indigenous communities, industry, post-
secondary institutions and career organizations.




46                                                                            Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025
                                                                                               RESULTS ANALYSIS


The Dual Credit Review Advisory Group gathered input on Alberta’s current and future dual credit programs,
including:
• the dual credit funding model and programming;
• accessibility, transportation and equipment barriers;
• transferability between post-secondary institutions;
• the role of collegiate schools in dual credit delivery;
• course offerings and instructional models; and
• increasing access to off-campus education.

Engagement between June and August 2024 focused on funding, delivery of off-campus education,
sustainability of career education programs, career exploration and transition supports to help students succeed
after high school. The department received positive feedback on the benefits of dual credit programming and
the need to plan for sustainability and growth of the program. Key themes include:
• improving clarity in policy documents;
• enhancing opportunities through sustainable funding;
• stronger connections between schools and the workplace; and
• improving awareness and participation from students, post-secondary institutions and businesses of existing
  career education opportunities.

Input gathered will inform upcoming changes to career education programming.

Dual Credit Programming
Dual credit programming is designed to help students in Grades 10, 11 or 12 gain practical skills and
experiential, hands-on learning while earning credits toward their high school diploma or a post-secondary
diploma, degree or certificate (including a journeyperson certificate). School authorities have the flexibility to
develop and implement dual credit programming that offers meaningful learning experiences that are relevant
to their students.
In 2024-25, Alberta Education invested almost $4.4 million into dual credit funding. This funding supports
up to $50,000 for each Dual Credit Start-up Grant to support school authority start-up costs for new dual
credit programs and up to $100,000 for each Dual Credit Enhancement Grant to enhance existing dual credit
programs. New or enhanced dual credit opportunities include carpentry and welding apprenticeships and
training in agriculture, engineering and medical and health technologies. Enhancement grant funding can be
used to purchase new equipment or upgrade facilities to meet industry standards and better connect students
to today’s workforce. Seniors, Community and Social Services also contributed an additional $600,000 for
health care aide dual credit grants. Across the province, 60 school authorities received 79 grants (57 start-up
grants and 22 enhancement grants). As of August 2024, approximately 2,600 students participated in dual
credit programs offered in 166 schools. Student participation numbers for 2024-25 will be available in fall
2025.
School Shared Resource Integration Program
To increase student participation in off-campus programs and internships, Alberta Education worked in
collaboration with Advanced Education and Jobs, Economy and Trade to support CAREERS: The Next
Generation, a not-for-profit foundation that brings together industry, schools, government and communities to
support students by making the workplace an extension of the classroom. High school students enrolled in off-



Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025                                                                           47
RESULTS ANALYSIS


campus education programming gain hands-on experience through work placement opportunities. In turn,
teachers, school authorities and industry employers that work with students receive off-campus supports from
the program. Under this program model, student career coordinators are employed by the school authority
and supported by CAREERS.
Alberta Education continued to support CAREERS in delivering its School Shared Resource Integration
Program, providing $1.4 million in 2024-25. In July 2024, the project expanded to include francophone
regional authorities and independent (private) schools. CAREERS highlighted that program participation
increased from 33 school authorities in 2023-24 to 45 school authorities in 2024-25. Approximately 2,400
students participated in an internship from August 2023 to August 2024.

Promoting Career Education Teacher Training
Targeted professional learning opportunities for teachers and system leaders help promote and enhance
career education programming for students and ensure they can experience a range of career pathways. In
March 2025, Alberta Education provided an 18-month $500,000 grant to the College of Alberta School
Superintendents to provide leadership to the Alberta Professional Learning Consortium to develop and
deliver career education professional learning to teachers and increase their capacity to teach career education
programming. Professional learning opportunities and resources will be delivered in both English and French
through in-person and online sessions and modules.

3.2   Explore strategies to enhance the apprenticeship system for students interested in pursuing
      training in the skilled trades, in partnership with other ministries.

As Alberta’s economy continues to grow, the demand for skilled tradespeople is increasing and will continue
to rise over the next decade. Meeting the needs of industry and maximizing opportunities for Alberta students
means promoting and supporting trades education with all communities. Alberta Education continues to
collaborate with Advanced Education, post-secondary institutions and industry associations to facilitate
student learning opportunities in apprenticeship programs, skilled trades and vocational education.

Exploring Germany’s Career Education Model
In June 2024, the Minister of Education led a mission to Germany to meet with political representatives and
officials from the states of Hesse, Rhineland-Palatinate, Schleswig-Holstein and Berlin. The delegation visited
schools, vocational centres and industry sites to see first-hand how Germany’s approach to career education is
helping students find rewarding careers. The mission focused on informing the Alberta government’s efforts to
expand career education for students. It also reinforced a long-standing relationship between governments to
ensure students, teachers and administrators can benefit from continued cultural exchanges and ideas sharing.
Education partners who joined the minister included representatives from CAREERS: The Next Generation
Foundation as well as the chairs and a second representative from each of The Edmonton Catholic Separate
School Division, The Edmonton School Division, The Calgary School Division and The Canadian Rockies
School Division.
Apprenticeship Programs
The Registered Apprenticeship Program allows students to develop trade-specific skills while earning high
school credits and on-the-job training hours toward an apprenticeship. Apprenticeship training combines
technical training (i.e., formal instruction in a designated post-secondary institution or high school) and on-
the-job training at a place of employment.




48                                                                             Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025
                                                                                               RESULTS ANALYSIS


Alberta Education continued to partner with Alberta Agriculture and Irrigation in support of the Green
Certificate Program through a five-year agreement, valid from 2022 to 2027, which allocates $400,000
per year to help facilitate increased Green Certificate Program accessibility, equity and student participation.
The program offers apprenticeship-style training to enable high school students to gain experience and
certification in Alberta’s agriculture industry while earning credit towards an Alberta High School Diploma
or a Certificate of High School Achievement. As of June 2024, about 996 students have participated in the
program, a 15 per cent increase over the previous school year. Student participation numbers for 2024-25
will be available in fall 2025.

3.3   Continue to support in-school opportunities to create more pathways for students to post-		
      secondary education and the workforce.

Collegiate schools provide students with opportunities to develop in-demand skills, equipping students
with technical experience, knowledge and critical-thinking skills. Increasing collegiate school opportunities
ensures a variety of career pathways for students to enter post-secondary education or the workforce. Alberta
Education’s commitment to expand collegiate programming across the province will connect more students to
rewarding career pathways while giving them the skills and abilities needed to be successful after high school
graduation.
Collegiate schools are operated by school authorities in partnership with the province’s post-secondary
institutions and industry partners. Through these partnerships, students receive enhanced career education
as they are connected to apprenticeship programming, allowing them to experience off-campus education
opportunities at post-secondary institutions and gain post-secondary credits while in high school.
Alberta Education continued to expand access to collegiate programs through an investment of $23.7
million in 2024-25 to support the start-up and expansion costs for collegiate schools. Through this funding,
seven new collegiate school programs began operations during the 2024-25 school year, increasing the total
number of collegiate schools operating in the province to 12. More than 30 unique collegiate pathways are
now available to students, including programming in science, aviation, technology, engineering, agriculture,
business, information communications and trades.
In November 2024, Education made several improvements to streamline the collegiate school application
process, including updating policy to require a partnership between collegiate schools, post-secondary
institutions and industry partners, establishing different collegiate structures, combining a multi-stage
application process into a single application, establishing clear parameters for school funding allocation and
introducing mandatory reporting requirements. Improvements will help reduce duplication of effort and
increase flexibility in collegiate requirements to support diverse programming.
The updated application process was implemented for the 2024-25 collegiate intake period. Department staff
continued to support collegiate school applicants and work with previously approved applicants to support
programs as they commenced operations.

Outcome Three Performance Measures

Performance Measure 3.a:
      Career Education Opportunities for Students

Rationale
• Giving students opportunities to explore different careers helps them make better choices for their future.
  With strong career planning, students are more ready to join the workforce or continue their education
  after high school.

Target
• The target was met, as the current result is not significantly different from the target value.

Discussion
• Overall survey results in 2024-25 indicate that 71.5 per cent of high school students, parents, teachers
  and school board trustees were satisfied with students’ opportunities to learn career education at school
  (Chart 3.a).
• Results overall have decreased over time but have remained stable compared to the previous year.
• Student and parent satisfaction have been stable over time for all four related survey questions.
• Teacher satisfaction has decreased over time for all four related survey questions. The survey of teachers was
  conducted online in 2021-22, 2022-23, 2023-24, and 2024-25, whereas previously it was administered by
  telephone. This change in methodology may have affected the results. Caution should be exercised when
  comparing results over time.
• School board trustees’ satisfaction that students in their jurisdiction can access academic counselling
  services in a timely manner at school when needed has been stable over time.

      writing, Mathematics, Science and Social Studies.”
•     For parents, teachers, school board trustees and the public, percentages reported are averages of agreement levels that “the
      curriculum focuses on what children/students need to learn in reading, writing, Mathematics, Science and Social Studies.”
•     The survey of teachers was conducted online from 2021-22 onwards, whereas previously it was administered by telephone,
      and this may have affected results. Caution should be exercised when comparing results overtime.
•     Overall satisfaction is the average of agreement/satisfaction levels for each respondent group. See Endnote A, page 82, for
      information on calculating overall survey results.
•     See Endnote A, pages 80-81, for information on surveys conducted for Alberta Education.

RESULTS ANALYSIS


Outcome Four: Alberta’s K–12 education system and workforce are well-managed
Alberta Education oversees the administration of the Early Childhood Services to Grade 12 education system
and directs the education system through legislation, policy and provision of funds. Maintaining a well-
governed and well-managed Alberta education system was achieved by:
• working with school authorities on recruitment and retention strategies for teachers and other school staff
  in underserved areas;
• addressing classroom complexities through specialized learning supports and improved access to qualified
  professionals and educational assistants;
• promoting choice in education to enhance student learning;
• implementing school capital strategies to increase the number of classrooms in areas of greatest need; and
• continuing to create efficiencies within the education system by streamlining financial and governance
  practices.

Key Objectives
4.1   Work with school authorities on recruitment and retention strategies for teachers and other school
      staff in underserved areas.

Alberta Education continued to explore ways to support the recruitment and retention of teachers and
other school staff by implementing strategies such as certificate and bursary programs that recruit to targeted
subject areas and underserved regions, increasing professional development opportunities, improving supports
for francophone and French language teachers, providing supports for school bus drivers and engaging in
education partnerships.

Teacher Certificate and Bursary Programs
Alberta Education continued to support the growth and development of the teaching workforce through
teacher certificate and bursary programs.

Northern Student Teacher Bursary
The Northern Student Teacher Bursary provides bursaries to Bachelor of Education students who agree
to return to service as a teacher within a northern Alberta school authority. Program participants are also
potential candidates for northern jurisdictions looking to fill positions. Through the program, which is also
supported by a contribution from Jobs, Economy and Trade, students can receive up to $18,000 to cover
their post-secondary education expenses. In 2024-25, this bursary was awarded to 37 recipients.

Rural Practicum Bursary
The Rural Practicum Bursary helps rural school authorities recruit new teachers by helping education
practicum students explore employment opportunities in rural school authorities. The program helps reduce
costs for students who complete practicum studies in rural areas. The program provides $2,000 bursaries for
rural practicum placements and $4,000 to students who complete a final field experience placement in Fort
Chipewyan. In 2024-25, the Rural Practicum Bursary program awarded 83 bursaries for a total of $166,000.




58                                                                            Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025
                                                                                           RESULTS ANALYSIS


Bridge to Teacher Certification
The Career and Technology Studies (CTS) Bridge to Teacher Certification Program provides an opportunity
for school authorities to recruit skilled individuals with qualifications aligned to the CTS Program of Study
clusters, alongside journeypersons, health-care professionals and information technologists. The program
authorizes certificated journeypersons, health-care professionals and information technologists to work as
teachers while pursuing a Bachelor of Education degree. Under the CTS bridging program of study, participants
who complete a full 36 credits in specified education courses are authorized to teach under a conditional Letter
of Authority until they graduate from a full Bachelor of Education degree program. Individuals graduating from
this four-year program will receive a Bachelor of Education, which permits them to teach with full scope within
Alberta’s schools.
In 2024-25, the department provided approximately $625,000 in funding to support 25 tradespeople to
qualify for a Letter of Authority while completing a university bridging program.

Professional Development Opportunities
Alberta Education continued to support the growth and development of the teaching workforce by providing
professional development opportunities. In 2024-25, Alberta Education:
• allocated a total of $90,000 to 90 individuals through the Math Bursary Program to support current and
  future teachers to enhance their knowledge, skills and confidence in teaching math;
• continued to support the growth of teacher capacity through Heritage Language Grants by providing
  $92,000 in grant funding to the International and Heritage Language Association and $174,000 in grant
  funding to the Southern Alberta Heritage Language Association; and
• approved five Alberta certificated teachers of international languages to take summer language courses at a
  recognized post-secondary institution outside of Canada, providing up to $4,200 each in funding through
  the International Language Teacher Bursary.
   - The bursary supports Kindergarten to Grade 12 French and international language teachers, language
     consultants and school administrators in improving their language proficiency, teaching skills and
     knowledge through an accredited education experience and helps Alberta Kindergarten to Grade 12
     school jurisdictions enhance their language teaching capacity.

Support for French Language Educators
Alberta Education remains committed to supporting the province’s growing francophone community
by delivering high-quality French-language education and other initiatives to help promote francophone
cultures. The Individual Teacher Bursary Program provides eligible teachers in Alberta up to $4,000 each year
for post-secondary courses (including master’s and doctoral programs), training and professional development
in French or related to French-language learning, education or pedagogy. The program is financed with federal
funding provided through the Canada-Alberta Agreement for Minority-Language Education and Second-
Language Instruction. In 2024-25, Alberta Education provided approximately $173,550 in Individual
Teacher Bursaries to 112 teachers.
Alberta Education led negotiations with the federal government to ratify the Canada-Alberta Agreement
for Minority-Language Education and Second-Language Instruction, securing over $99 million in federal
funding for Alberta over the next four years to support Early Childhood Services to Grade 12 and post-
secondary French-language education. Under the new protocol, additional funding will be provided for
minority-language and second-language teacher recruitment and retention and post-secondary minority-
language education.

Strengthening Supports Through Working Groups
Alberta Education led two separate working groups aimed at addressing challenges and strengthening supports
for French language educators. Both projects were undertaken through federal funding provided for teacher
recruitment and retention initiatives under the Official Languages in Education Program.
A working group that focused on professional development for teaching in a minority-language context
finished its work in April 2024. Strategies for the successful integration of new teachers teaching in a minority
setting were shared with school authorities and continued collaboration was encouraged.
A working group on staff French-language proficiency assessment ended in May 2024. A focus group
comprised of teachers, school administrators, human resources staff and consultants from school authorities
across Alberta met in December 2024 to provide feedback on the draft Guide to Supporting the French
Language Proficiency of Teachers: Engaging in Career-Long Learning. The feedback collected will be used to
inform the final version of the guide, which will support school authorities in providing formative feedback
and comprehensive language development recommendations to teachers and support their continuous
growth.

Education Partnerships
Alberta Education continues to identify opportunities to enhance subject matter expertise in the teaching
workforce by collaborating with other jurisdictions, relevant institutions and organizations.

Educator Exchange Programs
Educator exchange programs help build the capacity of teachers and school leaders by providing them with
opportunities to work in new and different environments and hone a wider variety of skills. These educator
exchanges are implemented in collaboration with the Alberta Teachers’ Association through an annual grant
of $135,000. In 2024-25, the department continued to offer virtual educator exchange programs with
Australia, Germany, Mexico and Spain; as well as in-person exchanges with Australia, Iceland, Germany
and Spain, to enhance the knowledge and skills of Alberta teachers and school leaders. Alberta Education
also continues to offer education exchange programs through Alberta’s accredited international schools.

Special Language Advisors
Alberta Education collaborated with partner governments and agencies under agreements for the posting of
special language advisors to Alberta, who work to support language teaching and learning in the education
system. In 2024-25, agreements for special language advisors were in place with China, Germany, Japan,
Spain and Ukraine. Special language advisors support professional development opportunities for Alberta
educators in language and culture programs, and in bilingual programs. They also work to support educational
exchanges and school partnership opportunities. Alberta Education allocated $170,000 to support advisors in
2024-25.

Research Partnerships Program
The department continues to facilitate collaborative research between school authorities and post-secondary
institutions through the Research Partnerships Program by providing funding for research projects with
potential to impact Alberta’s Early Childhood Services to Grade 12 education system and improve learning
outcomes for students.
The 2024 call for proposals funded five research studies on various topics, including aligning generative AI,
ethics and teachers’ values; engaging diverse stakeholders to advance equity in education; student readiness
for the future; social-emotional wellness in campus programs; and impacts of equitable assessments and



60                                                                             Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025
                                                                                           RESULTS ANALYSIS


reporting practices on high school students’ learning. Research teams provided quarterly research updates
and submitted research reports to Alberta Education and Alberta Research Network members through the
Research Partnerships Program Findings webinar series and an annual Alberta Research Network Symposium
held in November 2024. The webinars and symposiums bring together school authorities, universities and
educational partner organizations to network and share research and ideas.
In October 2024, the Research Partnerships Program amended its grant options for the 2025 call for
proposals to include two grant streams for a total of $250,000 to better meet the financial needs of research
teams and to fund a variety of studies. The Strategic Opportunity Grant stream offers funding for small- to
medium-scale studies with shorter timelines of up to two years while the Comprehensive Grant stream offers
funding for large-scale studies with longer timelines of up to three years.

College of Alberta School Superintendents
Alberta Education continues to support the College of Alberta School Superintendents (CASS) as a key
partner in Alberta’s education system. In January 2025, $591,040 in conditional grant funding was provided
to CASS to support deliverables aligned with the department’s priorities and mandate items through system
leadership.
Alberta Education also provides annual funding to CASS to support teachers and school leaders’ access to
professional learning opportunities through the Alberta Professional Learning Consortium. In 2024-25,
$4.34 million was provided to deliver professional learning sessions. Between February 2024 and January
2025, more than 107,000 educators attended approximately 3,100 sessions to support planning, instruction
and assessment practices for new curriculum implementation.

4.2    Address classroom complexities through specialized learning supports and improved access to
       qualified professionals and educational assistants.

Alberta Education supported school authorities in addressing classroom complexities during the 2024-25
school year by supporting diverse student demographics, providing low incidence supports and services,
improving testing and providing specialized assessments, expanding capacity and learner supports and
providing learning support funding.

Supporting Diverse Student Demographics
In 2024-25, Alberta Education provided $1 million to Seniors, Community and Social Services to support
the Wellness, Resiliency and Partnership 2.0: Fetal Alcohol Spectrum Disorder (FASD) Coaching Partnership
Project to strengthen the capacity of school staff in supporting students with FASD. The program fosters
collaboration between school authorities and FASD networks and offers tools and coaching support to help
educators create inclusive learning environments that support and engage students with FASD.
Low Incidence Supports and Services Funding
In 2024-25, $5 million of targeted funding was provided to 69 school authorities through Low Incidence
Supports and Services funding to facilitate access to educational programming for students with low incidence
disabilities, including students who are blind or visually impaired, deaf or hard of hearing, deaf-blind and
those with complex communication needs. As part of low incidence supports and services, Alberta Education
provided four Travelling Low Vision Clinics, three in Edmonton and one in Red Deer. A Travelling Low
Vision Clinic was also held in Calgary for May 2025. Travelling Low Vision Clinics provide comprehensive
low vision assessments to support educational programming to students.




Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025                                                                       61
RESULTS ANALYSIS


Improving Testing and Providing Specialized Assessments
Alberta Education recognizes that children with complex needs face multiple, interconnected challenges that
significantly affect their well-being and functioning across various settings, including schools, homes and
communities.
The Enhancing Specialized Assessment Grant was provided to Mandel & Associates Ltd., a Calgary-based
group of psychologists specialized in provision of expert assessments in the areas of clinical psychology,
educational psychology, neuropsychology, rehabilitation psychology and vocational psychology to coordinate
the administration of specialized assessments from October 2022 to April 2024. Mandel & Associates Ltd.
worked with qualified professionals to schedule and complete specialized assessments requested by public,
separate, francophone and charter school authorities. In April 2024, a final report was provided to Alberta
Education indicating that the grant supported the assessments of 3,227 children across Alberta. Findings
from the final report were shared with partner ministries and are being used to inform future work to support
children with complex needs.

Expanding Capacity and Learner Support Grant
Alberta Education provided $5.3 million in funding to the Association of Independent Schools and Colleges
in Alberta to operate the Expanding Capacity and Learner Support Grant Program. This program strengthens
the capacity of independent (private) schools and independent (private) Early Childhood Service operators
to deliver targeted supports and services to students with specialized learning needs. This includes providing
a variety of support such as resources, consultation, professional learning, and direct service and assessments
when required, to inform the child or student’s education program. The program now includes providing
support for home education families. The association indicates that there has been a steady increase in
service usage.

Program Supports and Services Grants
Alberta Education continues to promote inclusive education where diversity and learner differences are
embraced, and equitable learning opportunities are available to all students. In the 2024-25 school year,
learning support funding includes:
• $596.2 million allocated to the Specialized Learning Supports Grant for school jurisdictions to provide
  a continuum of specialized supports and services to students in an inclusive learning environment;
• $116 million allocated to the English as an Additional Language Grant and Francisation Grant to support
  school authorities or francophone regional authorities with 95,737 children or students who require
  additional support in the English or French language; and
• $57.5 million allocated to the Refugee Student Grant to help school authorities provide additional
  language, social and educational supports to 11,137 refugee students.

Program Unit Funding
A total of $219.9 million in learning support funding will be allocated to Program Unit Funding (PUF)
in the 2024-25 school year, an increase of $17.7 million from the previous year. Alberta Education provides
educational supports through PUF to school authorities, including independent (private) Early Childhood
Services operators, to support children aged two years, eight months to six years who have severe disabilities or
delays requiring support beyond what is offered in regular Early Childhood Services programs. Providing early
intervention services to eligible children across the province will help ensure they have a successful transition
into their early elementary years.




62                                                                             Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025
                                                                                               RESULTS ANALYSIS


Alberta Education reviewed the PUF Grant to ensure the program parameters and funding mechanism are
addressing the educational needs of children with severe disabilities or delays and to strengthen alignment with
the Special Education Coding Criteria and Funding Manual for School Authorities. As a result of the review, two
enrolment count dates, December and February, were implemented during the 2024-25 school year to ensure
all eligible children were enrolled and funded through the program.

Classroom Complexity Grant
The Classroom Complexity Grant allocated $42.5 million in learning support funding for the 2024-25
school year. The grant is provided to school authorities to address classroom complexity by increasing
staff hours, providing training and hiring more frontline support staff and specialists, such as counsellors,
psychologists and interpreters.
Approximately $480,000 of this grant will be available for the Educational Assistant Internship Program
to support training for current and aspiring educational assistants. Created in 2023 in partnership with The
Edmonton School Division, the program helps address the provincial shortage of educational assistants by
providing flexible professional development and training programming to educational assistants. The program
fosters an educational assistant community of practice and provides on-site coaching, oversight and research.
Program participants can engage in on-the-job training and learn educational assistant theory and skills
while being employed, enabling participants without post-secondary training to join the educational assistant
workforce. 281 individuals have participated in the program to date, with 400 people participating in 2025-26.

4.3   Promote choice in education to enhance student learning.

Providing school choice contributes to the success of Alberta’s education system and offers parents the
opportunity to choose the educational path that best supports their child’s unique needs. The department
recognizes that public, separate, francophone, independent (private) and charter schools, as well as Early
Childhood Services programs and home education programs are integral to providing a variety of options for
students and parents.
Education system funding was increased to almost $9.3 billion in 2024-25, ensuring that the Kindergarten to
Grade 12 education system continued to support children and students to develop their knowledge and skills
and achieve learning outcomes.

Charter Schools
Alberta Education continues to expand options to provide choice in education through charter schools. In the
2024-25 school year, the ministry allocated approximately $162 million for operating funds. Charter schools
are autonomous, non-profit, publicly funded schools that offer provincial curriculum in an innovative or
enhanced way to improve student learning and contribute to educational choice.
In 2024-25, charter schools had the option to be funded through a new Specialized Learning Support Grant
or remain on the Severe Disabilities Grant. Alberta Education engaged with stakeholders in fall 2024 to explore
issuing charters on a continuing basis rather than operating on a term basis as presently described in legislation.

Independent (Private) Schools
School choice is also supported through independent (private) schools and independent (private) Early
Childhood Services operators. Independent (private) schools receive 70 per cent of the base instruction
funding provided to public schools and are not eligible for capital funding.
In 2024-25, Alberta Education provided $428.8 million to accredited funded independent (private) schools,
an increase of $35.1 million from the prior year, reflective of growing enrolment in these schools.



Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025                                                                           63
RESULTS ANALYSIS


Alberta Education also provided approximately $340,000 to the Association of Independent Schools and
Colleges in Alberta in 2024-25 to provide teacher evaluation and certification services to independent
(private) schools, independent (private) Early Childhood Services operators and Alberta accredited
international schools.

Home Education
Home education is a parent-directed approach to educating a student in Grades 1 to 12 at home or elsewhere
in which parents are responsible for making all education decisions. Parents choose curriculum resources and
methodology consistent with their family’s beliefs and the Home Education Regulation. There are two types
of home education programs in Alberta: supervised by a school authority, which is eligible for funding, and
not supervised by a school authority, which is not eligible for funding.
The Home Education Grant is provided to public, separate, francophone and accredited funded independent
(private) school authorities to support students in a home education program. Up to 50 per cent of the Home
Education Grant is provided to the parent/guardian as reimbursement for instructional materials and services
that support the instructional program at home. Parents in a supervised home education program can also use
their portion of parent funding to access online/distance education courses.
In 2024-25, Alberta Education continued to provide $1,802 per student through Home Education Grant
funding. There are currently over 21,500 students enrolled in home education programs in Alberta.

Home Education Kindergarten Pilot Program
The Home Education Kindergarten pilot program was created in the 2023-24 school year to expand
educational choices for parents and to assess the interest and feasibility of home education for Kindergarten-
aged children.
In the 2024-25 school year, the pilot allocated $1.8 million in funding to support up to 2,048 children,
allowing parents providing home education to their Kindergarten children to access funding through the
temporary grant program. A grant of $901 per child was allocated to supervising school authorities, including
public, separate, francophone and independent (private) school authorities. Up to $450.50 was designated for
parental reimbursement for instructional materials and resources.
Student Transportation
Student transportation is an integral part of Alberta’s education system. Under the Education Act and School
Transportation Regulation, school boards are required to provide transportation to their designated school
for their resident students who meet the distance criteria. School authorities receive transportation funding
to support the safe transportation of more than 335,000 Kindergarten to Grade 12 students across
the province each school day.
In the 2024-25 fiscal year, Alberta Education provided $439.7 million in transportation funding to school
jurisdictions and $25.9 million to accredited independent (private) schools and independent (private) Early
Childhood Services operators.
Alberta Education also offered transportation support to school authorities through the Fuel Price
Contingency Program, helping to offset the impact of fuel price increases for school authorities by keeping
fuel prices at a base rate of $1.25 per litre. In 2024-25, more than $16 million was provided to school
authorities to support high fuel costs.




64                                                                            Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025
                                                                                            RESULTS ANALYSIS


School Bus Driver Grant
The School Bus Driver Grant compensates school authorities and new school bus drivers for the time it takes
to complete their training to help address concerns about driver shortages, recruitment and retention. The
grant has provided $8.5 million to support the training of new Class 2 and Class 4 school bus drivers since
it was introduced in April 2023. School authorities received $4.7 million in funding for 1,315 drivers who
completed training from April 2024 to March 2025.

4.4    Implement school capital strategies to increase the number of classrooms in areas of greatest need.

To keep pace with rising student enrolment, Alberta Education invested in new schools and delivered
additional student spaces in the fastest-growing communities for students to learn, grow and reach their
full potential. Through Budget 2024, Alberta Education provided $2.8 billion for school construction as a
three-year capital investment, funding 99 school projects in various stages across the province. Approximately
$140 million was also provided for the production and distribution of modular classrooms for the 2024-25
school year, resulting in the creation of an additional 4,550 student spaces and the relocation of more than
700 student spaces to schools with the greatest space needs.
In 2024-25, Alberta Education:
• advanced 31 priority school projects, including 23 new schools, three replacement schools and five
  modernizations;
• prioritized 15 school projects in the Calgary metro area and 11 school projects in the Edmonton metro
  area, creating over 23,000 new or updated student spaces;
• approved full construction funding for 18 priority school projects; and
• built 12 schools in 11 communities across the province, with the new schools operating in Blackfalds,
  Camrose, Coaldale, Edmonton, Fort Vermillion, Grande Prairie, Langdon, Leduc, Manning, Red Deer
  and Sherwood Park. These 12 capital projects added more than 11,000 new and updated student spaces.

School Construction Accelerator Program
The School Construction Accelerator Program was announced in September 2024. Through the program,
Alberta Education committed an additional $8.6 billion for school construction to create new and updated
student spaces. This will deliver more than 100 new and updated schools over the next seven years. The
program, together with previously approved school projects and an additional $150 million over three years
to supply modular classrooms, will deliver approximately 200,000 new and modernized spaces for students
over the next seven years.
The School Construction Accelerator Program speeds up the construction of schools by approving school
projects for their next stage in the construction process in-year, allowing projects to move through planning,
design and construction stages without having to wait until the next budget cycle for approval, reducing
project timelines by as much as six months. Through this change, and funded as part of the $8.6 billion
school capital investment, 22 previously announced priority school projects across the province were approved
to move from one stage to the next in the construction funding process. Of the 22 projects, 17 were approved
to move from the design stage to full construction funding. Eight of the new school projects will be in
Edmonton, four in Calgary and one each in Airdrie, Chestermere, Okotoks, Barrhead, Breton, Mallaig,
Spruce Grove, Taber, Wainwright and Redcliff, creating or updating more than 21,000 student spaces.
The funding will also accommodate increased demand for charter school programming and their space needs
by allowing Alberta Education to add 12,500 new charter school student spaces over the next four years
through a Charter School Accelerator pilot program.


Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025                                                                        65
RESULTS ANALYSIS


4.5   Continue to create efficiencies within the education system by streamlining financial and
      governance practices.

Alberta Education continues to streamline governance and regulatory practices by reviewing legislation,
policies, forms and other applicable documents to identify opportunities for reducing regulatory requirements.
In 2024-25, Alberta Education provided guidance and clarity to school authorities and created efficiencies
within the education system.

Education Amendment Act, 2024
The Education Act governs Alberta’s education system and identifies the roles and responsibilities of the
Minister, school boards, charter schools and independent (private) schools, teachers, parents and students.

The Education Amendment Act, 2024 received Royal Assent on December 5, 2024. The resulting changes to
the Education Act support families and students navigating complex conversations around gender identity,
sexual orientation and human sexuality, while also supporting continuity in a student’s learning during a
public health emergency or state of emergency.

Changes that came into effect on March 1, 2025, reinforce the importance of students continuing to have
access to education during emergencies. These changes also provide more transparency for parents about
health measures in schools and more guidance and clarity for teachers and school administrators on matters
such as shifts to at-home learning during public emergencies.

Changes coming into effect on September 1, 2025, will increase transparency and strengthen communication
to parents. Changes relate to parental notification and consent when a student requests to use a new gender
identity-related preferred name or pronouns; parental notification and opt-in consent for classroom content
dealing primarily and explicitly with gender identity, sexual orientation or human sexuality; and Minister
approval of learning and teaching resources dealing primarily and explicitly with these topics as well as
external parties using or providing such resources in schools.

Superintendent of Schools Regulation
Superintendents play a fundamental role in enhancing school leadership and supporting quality teaching and
optimum learning for all students in their respective school authorities. To support the recruitment of school
leaders, the Superintendent of Schools Regulation was amended through a ministerial order in July 2024 to
align superintendent compensation with the salary increase to public sector management.

School Transportation Amendment Regulation
Recognizing that school transportation is an integral part of the education system, the department
announced changes to the School Transportation Regulation in February 2023, amending the distance criteria
requirements for student transportation eligibility. Following feedback from stakeholders regarding capacity
to meet new criteria, the distance eligibility criteria for Grades 1 to 6 students will be revised to 1.6 kilometres
effective September 1, 2025, instead of the previously announced one kilometre distance. The criteria for
Grades 7 to 12 students will be lowered to two kilometres, as initially announced. Transition funding will be
provided for the 2025-26 school year for school authorities that continue to transport Kindergarten to Grade
6 students who reside between one and 1.6 kilometres from their attending school.




66                                                                               Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025
                                                                                           RESULTS ANALYSIS


Revised Funding Framework
In February 2025, Alberta Education announced a revised funding model to be implemented in the 2025-
26 school year. The new funding allocation method will be based on a two-year average enrolment instead
of three, with 30 per cent of the funding based on the current school year’s enrolment and 70 per cent based
on the upcoming year’s projections. The model will allow the department to be more responsive to growing
school authorities and enable school authorities to access predictable and sustainable funding amid enrolment
increases while shielding authorities from declining enrolment.

Fair Dealing Compliance Program
Alberta Education led the Fair Dealing Compliance Program initiative in partnership with the Council of
Ministers of Education, Canada to provide guidance and reference materials to educators and administrators
for the use of copy-written material and was the main contact for the refund litigation. In response to 2012
changes in law and the decision of the Supreme Court of Canada interpreting fair dealing in an education
setting, the Council of Minsters of Education, Canada Copyright Consortium developed and published the
Fair Dealing Guidelines to help teachers and school administrators understand how to use copyright-protected
works appropriately. The Fair Dealing Guidelines apply to non-profit Kindergarten to Grade 12 schools and
post-secondary education institutions and provide reasonable safeguards for the owners of copyright-protected
works. Litigation matters concluded in spring 2024 with a victory for the participating provinces, territories
and school boards.

Red Tape Reduction
Alberta Education continues to streamline governance and regulatory practices by reviewing legislation,
policies, forms and other applicable documents to identify opportunities for reducing and streamlining
regulatory requirements. In 2024-25, Alberta Education:
• Brought forward minor and technical amendments to the Teaching Profession Act, which received Royal
  Assent on December 5, 2024.
   - Amendments made include administrative and technical corrections to ensure terminology regarding
     certificates and the way they are issued as referenced in the Teaching Profession Act align with the
     Education Amendment Act, 2024.

• Provided updated and streamlined forms to school authorities to enable simpler reporting to meet the
  terms and conditions of the Canada-Alberta Agreement for Minority-Language Education and Second-
  Language instruction.
   - The revised form is available on the Official Languages in Education Programs webpage, and a webinar
     was held on June 7, 2024, to answer stakeholder questions.

• Continued to implement digital and other innovative solutions to improve the delivery of essential
  government services and reduce administrative burden by providing non-regulatory ministerial orders for
  signing via electronic signature.
   - In 2024-25, Alberta Education prepared and received 34 electronically signed ministerial orders.

Performance Measure 4.a:
      Schools are Able to Attract and Retain Teachers

Rationale
• Attracting and retaining qualified teachers in schools is necessary for the delivery of high-quality education.
  Skilled and dedicated educators foster engaging learning environments and provide consistent support to
  students for their long-term academic success.

Target
• The target was not met.

Discussion
• Overall survey results in 2024-25 indicate that 70.3 per cent of principals agreed that they are able to
  attract and retain teachers at school (Chart 4.a).
• Results overall have decreased over time but have remained stable compared to the previous year.
• Principals’ results have decreased for both related survey questions.
• Principal surveys were administered online.

      - you are able to retain teachers in your school.
•     The target for 2024-25 was set in February 2024 based on 2022-23 data.
•     Overall agreement is the average of agreement levels for the respondent group. See Endnote A, page 82, for information on
      calculating overall survey results.
•     See Endnote A, pages 80-81, for information on surveys conducted for Alberta Education.

     Teacher Preparation

Rationale
• Teacher education training and professional development is important in maintaining the quality of the
  Kindergarten to Grade 12 education system.

Discussion
• Overall survey results in 2024-25 indicate that 68.3 per cent of teachers and school board trustees agreed
  that teachers are prepared for teaching.
• Results overall have decreased over time but have remained stable compared to the previous year.
• School board trustees’ results have been consistently higher than those of teachers.
• Teachers’ agreement has decreased over time for the following five survey questions:
     - teacher education program prepared them adequately to meet the Teaching Quality Standard for initial

     - recent Bachelor of Education graduates are well prepared to meet their responsibilities as teachers,
     - professional development opportunities effectively addressed teacher’s professional development needs,

  Caution should be exercised when comparing results over time.
• School board trustees’ agreement that recent Bachelor of Education graduates are well prepared to meet
  their responsibilities as teachers has decreased over time.

RESULTS ANALYSIS


Performance Measure 4.b:
      Safe, Caring and Healthy Learning Environment

Rationale
• A safe, caring and healthy learning environment is fundamental to facilitating student learning.

Target
• The target was not met.

Discussion
• In 2024-25, 83.6 per cent of students, parents, teachers and school board trustees agreed that schools
  provide a safe, caring and healthy learning environment (Chart 4.b).
• Results overall have decreased over time but have remained stable compared to the previous year.
• Students’ agreement that students treat each other well at school and that teachers care about students
  has decreased over time.
• Parent agreement that students feel safe at school or during remote learning; that students treat each other
  well at school; that students are treated fairly by adults at school; that students are learning how to stay
  healthy at school; and satisfaction that schools are contributing to students’ development of social skills as
  well as emotional well-being has decreased over time. In contrast, parent agreement that schools promote
  physical activity as well as the gym, playground and other facilities such as sports fields and fitness areas
  at their school meet the daily physical activity needs of students has increased over time.
• Teacher agreement that school promotes physical activity; and students have healthy food choices in the
  school cafeteria, canteen, vending machines, and at classroom and school events has remained stable over
  time. Teacher agreement has decreased over time for all other related survey questions. The survey of
  teachers was conducted online in 2021-22, 2022-23, 2023-24, and 2024-25, whereas previously it was
  administered by telephone. This change in methodology may have affected the results. Caution should
  be exercised when comparing results over time.
• Among the four respondent groups, parent and teacher agreement are lowest regarding students having
  healthy food choices in the school cafeteria, canteen, vending machines, and at classroom and school
  events.
• School board trustee agreement is lowest regarding school contributing to students’ development of
  emotional well-being.

RESULTS ANALYSIS


Supplementary Information
     Quality of Kindergarten to Grade 12 Education

Rationale
• High quality Kindergarten to Grade 12 education meets the needs of all students, society and the
  economy.

Discussion
• Overall survey results in 2024-25 indicate that 77.6 per cent of high school students, parents, teachers,
  school board trustees, and the public were satisfied with the overall quality of Kindergarten to Grade 12
  education.
• Results overall have decreased over time but have remained stable compared to the previous year.
• Public satisfaction continues to be lower compared to the satisfaction of other respondent groups.
• Student agreement that school work is challenging has decreased over time.
• Parent satisfaction with the quality of teaching at their child’s school has decreased over time. Similarly,
  parent agreement that the highest priority of Alberta’s education system is the success of the students has
  decreased over time. However, parent agreement that their child clearly understands what students are
  expected to learn at school has increased over time.
• Teacher satisfaction with the quality of education and the quality of teaching at their school has decreased
  over time. Similarly, teacher agreement that the highest priority of Alberta’s education system is the success
  of the student, that school work is interesting, and that students clearly understand what they are expected
  to learn at school has decreased over time. The survey of teachers was conducted online in 2021-22,
  2022-23, 2023-24, and 2024-25, whereas previously it was administered by telephone. This change in
  methodology may have affected the results. Caution should be exercised when comparing results over time.
• School board trustee satisfaction with the quality of education at schools in their jurisdiction has decreased
  over time. Similarly, school board trustee agreement that the highest priority of Alberta’s education system
  is the success of the student, and that students in their jurisdiction clearly understand what they are
  expected to learn at school has decreased over time.
• Public satisfaction with the quality of education at school in their community has decreased over time.

Performance Measure 4.c:
      Supports and Services

Rationale
• Accessing supports and services in a timely manner has an influence on student achievement and is one of
  the important factors for effective learning.

Target
• The target was not met.

Discussion
• Overall survey results in 2024-25 indicate that 72.9 per cent of high school students, parents, teachers, and school
  board trustees agreed that supports and services for students can be accessed in a timely manner (Chart 4.c).
• Results overall have decreased over time but have remained stable compared to the previous year.
• Results for parents and teachers are consistently lower than those of other respondent groups.
• Parent agreement was lowest regarding school helping struggling students keep up with their studies.
• Teacher agreement has decreased over time for all related survey questions. The survey of teachers was
  conducted online in 2021-22, 2022-23, 2023-24, and 2024-25, whereas previously it was administered by
  telephone. This change in methodology may have affected the results. Caution should be exercised when
  comparing results over time.
• Teacher agreement was lowest regarding timely access to:
     - services (beyond regular instruction) that help students read and write at school when needed; and
     - student assessment services for students with learning difficulties.
• School board trustee agreement has decreased over time for the following:
     - support services for students with disabilities can be accessed in a timely manner at school when needed; and
     - student assessment services for students with learning difficulties can be accessed in a timely manner at
       school when needed.

RESULTS ANALYSIS



Performance Measure and Indicator Methodology

Endnote A: Notes on Surveys

Provincial Satisfaction Surveys
Alberta Education regularly commissions telephone and online surveys of random samples of the Alberta
public and key education partners. The purpose of these surveys is to obtain perceptions of how the Early
Childhood Services to Grade 12 education system is performing in meeting students’ and society’s needs
and expectations. Professional survey research firms are contracted to administer the surveys, using trained
interviewers and a Computer-Assisted Telephone Interviewing system for telephone surveys, or using a
Computer-Assisted Web Interviewing system for online surveys.
Survey data is compiled by the survey research firm in SPSS format and provided to the ministry for analysis.
Surveys are conducted annually with the public, parents of Early Childhood Services to Grade 12 students,
high school students, teachers, principals, school board trustees, and business and industry employers.
Table 1 provides sampling information about the surveys of Albertans conducted in recent years that are
reported in this annual report. The information includes the respondent groups surveyed, the sample sizes
and the response rates. Also shown are the maximum width confidence intervals for results from each survey.
Results are considered accurate within the confidence interval 19 times out of 20 (i.e., at a confidence level
of 95 per cent). Survey fielding for 2024-25 began in mid-January and concluded in mid-May. The resultant
sample sizes and confidence intervals are shown in the table on page 81. While the table is useful as a rough
guide to the confidence intervals associated with survey results, a confidence interval for the difference is used
to determine whether the difference between two specific survey results (either over time or among respondent
groups) is statistically significant (i.e., likely a real difference, and not due to sampling variation).
The survey instruments for these surveys primarily use four-point response scales (Very Satisfied/ Satisfied/
Dissatisfied/Very Dissatisfied or Strongly Agree/Agree/Disagree/Strongly Disagree), depending on the
question. A few questions use other response scales (e.g., A Lot/Some/Very Little/Not at All or Improved/
Same/Declined). Although not asked, Don’t Know responses and refusals are recorded as well. Results
presented are the combined percentages of respondents who were Very Satisfied/Satisfied or who Strongly
Agreed/Agreed. The survey results are available at education provincial satisfaction surveys.

RESULTS ANALYSIS


Calculating Overall Survey Results
When calculating overall results across all respondent groups for survey measures, a simple average
(rather than a weighted average) is used to reflect each group’s equal importance to the overall result.
Further, although survey results for each respondent group and for overall results are rounded to whole
numbers, the overall average results are calculated from the unrounded result for each respondent group.
This avoids possible double rounding error that might result if overall averages were calculated from rounded
respondent group results.

Use of Confidence Intervals for Interpreting Survey and Survey Measure Results
When a result is obtained by surveying a random sample of the target population, such as the public, parents
or teachers, there is a confidence interval associated with the result, which is expressed as a percentage above
and below the obtained result. A confidence interval indicates how much variation one might expect from the
obtained survey result, as a consequence of sampling and diversity among respondents.
Three key factors influencing the degree of sampling variability associated with survey results are the sample
size of the surveys, the magnitude of the result, and the sample size relative to the size of the population:
• The larger the sample size, the smaller the associated confidence interval.
• Given a constant sample size, confidence intervals are smallest for survey percentages near one per cent
  and 99 per cent and largest for survey percentages near 50 per cent. This is because results near one per
  cent and 99 per cent represent maximum uniformity of the underlying population on the variable being
  measured by the survey (e.g., satisfaction/agreement on various educational issues), while results near
  50 per cent represent maximum diversity of the underlying population on the variable being measured.
• If the sample size is more than 5% of a finite population, then the confidence interval is reduced according
  to a Finite Population Correction Factor.
Table 1 illustrates the interaction of these three factors in determining the margin of error for results from
surveys. The surveys of parents, high school students, teachers and the public all used sample sizes between
600 and 803 in 2024-25 and survey results ranged from 91.2 per cent (for student satisfaction with the
opportunity to receive a solid grounding in core subjects) to 52.4 per cent (for teacher satisfaction with career
education opportunities for students). The margins of error range from 2.3 per cent to 3.9 per cent.
In addition, as survey measures generally combine survey results on multiple questions from multiple
respondent groups, a customized statistical algorithm is used to construct the confidence interval for measure
results.
Confidence intervals for specific results and specific measures are determined by the following procedures:
1. To determine the confidence interval for specific survey results for single respondent groups, an online
   statistical application (https://americanresearchgroup.com/moe.html) is used.
2. Differences in survey results between respondent groups are supported by specific tests using an online
   statistical application (https://sapioresearch.com/significant-difference-calculator/)
3. To determine the confidence interval for specific survey measures with multiple respondent groups,
   a customized statistical algorithm is used.




82                                                                              Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025
                                                                                                 RESULTS ANALYSIS


In the Ministry of Education’s annual report, confidence intervals are used to determine:
• whether differences in survey results or in survey measure results over time are likely the result of sampling
  variation or represent actual change; and
• whether differences in survey results among respondent groups in the same year are likely the result of
  sampling variation or represent actual differences.
When comparing results of survey questions over time or among respondent groups, it is concluded that
there is an actual difference between two survey results, 19 times out of 20, if the confidence interval for the
difference does not include zero. When this is the case, it is acknowledged in the related text that there is an
increase, change or significant difference in survey results over time or among groups. When the confidence
interval for the difference does include zero, the observed difference may be due to sampling variation, and we
cannot conclude that the difference is real or significant. In such cases, the related text indicates that results are
similar or stable over time or among respondent groups.

RESULTS ANALYSIS


Endnote B: Technical Notes

Assessing Results Relative to Targets
Performance targets set expectations for results within specific time frames and are the basis for planning
improvements and assessing results that are within the sphere of influence of the Ministry and school
authorities. They are an important way to gauge whether the education system is improving or falling behind
in obtaining desired results in key areas. Targets clearly establish desired levels of performance better than
recent performance, to be attained within a specific time frame. Targets are quantifiable and expressed in
numerical terms, such as percentages or ratios. They are used as a key tool to drive, measure, improve and
manage performance.
For survey measures, performance is considered to have met the target if the confidence interval for the
result includes the target value. For example, if a measure result is 77 per cent with a confidence interval
of 77 per cent ± 2.5 per cent and a target of 79 per cent, then the target has been met, since the target value
lies within the interval [74.5 per cent, 79.5 per cent]. If the result on the same measure is 75 per cent, with
a confidence interval of 75 per cent ± 2.5 per cent, then the target has not been met since the target value of
79 per cent does not lie within the interval [72.5 per cent, 77.5 per cent].
This method of assessing performance represents a high level of achievement in relation to the target and
considers the sampling variability in the survey process.
For provincial assessments and student outcome measures (e.g., high school completion rate), a one sample
Chi-square statistical test is used to determine whether the result is significantly different from the target.
The target is met when results are not significantly different from the target. The magnitude of the difference
required between the result and the target to establish significance depends on the sample size, for example,
the number of students writing an exam.

Assessing Results Over Time
Changes in results over time are assessed in the discussion of each measure. For survey measures, which
are based on probability sampling, confidence intervals are considered. See Use of Confidence Intervals
for Interpreting Survey and Survey Measure Results in Endnote A on page 82, for an explanation of how
confidence intervals are used to assess changes over time for survey measures.
Changes over time are assessed with a two sample Chi-square statistical test for measures based on tests of
student learning or on administrative data, i.e., derived from tracking groups of students over time, such
as dropout and high school completion rates. The test determines whether the difference between two
proportions (e.g., base year and current year results) is significant. The calculation of Chi-square is based on
the difference between what was observed and what would be expected assuming that the proportions were
the same. Under a simple test of proportions, a Chi-square value of “1” represents a difference of one standard
deviation, while a Chi-square value of 3.84 is significant at the five per cent level. Discussion of results over
time for the non-survey measures is supported by this test of significant difference.

Trend Lines
Although not appearing in the annual report, trend lines are an aid for interpreting the results for measures
reported in the annual report. Trend lines augment data interpretation techniques already in use, such as the
calculation of confidence intervals, and are used to determine the significance of changes in measure results
over time.




84                                                                             Education | Annual Report 2024 – 2025
                                                                                               RESULTS ANALYSIS


Trend lines are used to develop an expected value for a current result given historical results. The expected
value can then be compared with the actual result. In this way, trend lines provide a useful method of
understanding year-to-year fluctuations and shift the focus from smaller year-to-year changes by providing
information about the direction of results over the longer term. It is of interest whether results are tracking
towards the performance target for the measures that have not met targets.
Discussion of results over time for measures with at least three data points are supported by trend lines. The
type of trend line used is selected based on an informed interpretation of the data series underlying the trend.
Logarithmic trend lines are appropriate for proportional data, i.e., data expressed as percentages of a whole and
therefore ranging between zero per cent and 100 per cent, such as the performance measures in this report. A
logarithmic trend line is a best-fit straight line that is most useful when the rate of change in the data increases
or decreases quickly, then levels out. The leveling out can be seen in the results for some measures in the
report, along with examples of year-to-year changes in other measures.
A logarithmic trend line is calculated using the following formula:
Y = cln(x) + b, where c and b are constants and ln( ) is the natural logarithm function and x is the year of the
series.

RESULTS ANALYSIS


Endnote C: Methodology for Performance Measures
Described below are the methodologies used to calculate the measures that are derived from provincial
assessments of student learning and from other information about Alberta students in the Ministry of
Education’s data systems.

Definition of Alberta Student
An Alberta student is an individual who is registered as of September 30 of the school year in a school located
in Alberta, where “school” means a structured learning environment through which an education program
is offered to a student by: a board (includes public, separate, francophone, Lloydminster); an operator of
an independent (private) school; an Early Childhood Services program private operator; the operator of a
charter school; a parent giving a home education program; the Minister. It also includes other Alberta-located
educational institutions providing Early Childhood Services to Grade 12 curriculum instruction, specifically
federal (First Nations’ schools and those operated by Corrections Canada); and provincial authorities (such as
Bow Valley College and NorQuest College).

Results for First Nations, Métis and Inuit Students
Calculating results for First Nations, Métis and Inuit students is possible because school authorities are required
to include a voluntary First Nations, Métis and Inuit self-identification question on school registration forms
and to report the identifier to the ministry’s student data system. Self-identification of students has increased
each year. Students’ First Nations, Métis and Inuit self-identification information associated with their most
recent school registration is used to calculate results. This provides the most complete results and reflects the
student’s intent to be identified each year.

Annual Dropout Rate of Students Aged 14-18
The annual dropout rate reports the percentages of Alberta students aged 14-18 in public, separate,
francophone, charter, and accredited independent (private) schools who, in the following school year:
• are not enrolled in the Kindergarten to Grade 12 system;
• are not enrolled in a post-secondary institution in Alberta;
• are not registered in an apprenticeship program in Alberta; and
• have not completed high school.
The annual dropout rate is derived from student data in Alberta Education and Alberta Advanced Education
systems. Adjustments for attrition are based on estimates from Alberta Demographic Estimates 48_CD_DR_
AG_2002_2024.xlsx and DMTI_December2024_Education.xlsx.
Alberta students who are identified as having a moderate or severe cognitive disability or a severe multiple
disability are not included in the annual dropout rate.
Annual dropout rate is calculated by dividing the number of students who have dropped out of school (adjusted
for attrition) by the number of 14- to-18-year-old students who were registered in the Kindergarten to Grade
12 system in the previous school year (the age specific cohort), less attrition as shown in the formula below:
Count of Students        Count of 14-18
